Weights decide the size of the file and how easily the network overfits. Dense1 holds 73,792 of the 80,604 weights, which is 92%. The three convolution layers together hold only 6,032, about 7.5%.

Multiplications decide the time one prediction takes. A convolution uses each of its weights at every position of its output map. Dense layers use each weight once.

LayerMultiplications for one photoHow it is countedShare
Conv1830,30462 x 62 positions x 8 filters x 27 weights32.7%
Conv2968,83229 x 29 x 16 x 7238.2%
Conv3663,55212 x 12 x 32 x 14426.2%
Dense173,7281,152 x 642.9%
Dense276864 x 120.03%
Total2,537,184about 2.5 million100%

So the picture flips: the convolution layers own 7.5% of the weights but do 97% of the multiplications. The first dense layer owns 92% of the weights but does 3% of the work.

Why does this split matter?

It tells me where to look for each kind of problem. When the network overfitted in my first attempt, the place to act was the dense layer, where the weights are; a smaller dense layer was part of the fix (chapter 8). When I wanted prediction to be fast on the Pi, the place to act was the convolution code, where the work is; that is why chapter 9 spends most of its time on making convolution fast.

Interview tip

I can rebuild the whole table from memory with two formulas: output = input - 3 + 1 for a convolution, halve for a pool, and weights = filters x (9 x input channels) + filters. Interviewers like to ask "how many parameters does your network have, and where are most of them?" The answer: "80,604. 92% are in the first dense layer, 1,152 by 64. But 97% of the multiplications are in the three convolution layers, because their few weights are reused at every position."

Design story

Version 1 used YOLOv5x, a network with about 86 million weights, a thousand times bigger than this one. It had to be: it found boxes and named 14 classes in a full 640-pixel frame, with several items at once. Version 2 names one item in a 64 x 64 crop. Making the task small was what made a network small enough to write, understand and check by hand.

8 How the CNN learns

Short version: the network starts with random weights and guesses badly. A number called the loss measures how bad. Backpropagation works out, for every one of the 80,604 weights, which way to nudge it to make the loss smaller. Gradient descent makes those nudges, 32 pictures at a time, for 40 passes over the training set. A gradient check proves my hand-written maths is right.

What training looks like

Before the how, here is the what. Figure 8.1 shows my training run. The horizontal axis counts epochs; one epoch is one full pass over all 1,290 training crops. The teal line is the accuracy on the training crops. The orange line is the accuracy on the 277 validation crops, which the network never trains on.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my run the best validation epoch was 34 of 40: training accuracy 98.1%, validation accuracy 93.5% (259 of 277). After epoch 1 the numbers were 21.4% and 26.0%; after epoch 10, 74.8% and 79.1%; after epoch 20, 92.1% and 89.8%. One epoch took about 45 s on my laptop's processor, so the whole run took about 30 minutes.

The rest of this chapter explains what happens inside one step of that climb, and why the curve has this shape.

The loss: one number for "how wrong"

To improve, the network needs a score for how wrong it currently is. That score is called the loss. A small loss means good predictions. Training is nothing more than changing the weights to make the loss smaller.

My loss is the cross-entropy loss. For one picture it looks only at the chance the network gave to the right class, and takes the negative natural logarithm of it:

loss for one picture = -ln(chance given to the right class)

The natural logarithm ln is the opposite of raising e to a power. All I need to know about it here is in this table.

Chance given to the right classLoss = -ln(chance)In words
0.990.01Sure and right: almost no loss
0.900.11Confident and right
0.500.69Undecided
0.083 (1 in 12)2.48Pure guessing among 12 classes
0.014.61Sure and wrong: a heavy penalty

The loss of a batch is the average over its pictures. The value 2.48 is the landmark to remember: it is the loss of a network that gives every class the same chance, 1 in 12. A fresh network with random weights does a little worse than that at first, because its random scores are uneven and it is confidently wrong about some pictures. Within the first epoch the loss drops below 2.48, and from there on the network is doing better than guessing.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my run the loss of the very first batch was about 4.9. The average training loss over epoch 1 was 2.31, and it fell to 0.07 by epoch 34. The validation loss at epoch 34 was 0.24.

Why not simply count the mistakes?

Counting mistakes (accuracy) is what I care about in the end, but it cannot guide learning. If a weight changes by a tiny amount, the count of mistakes almost always stays exactly the same, so it gives no hint about which direction is better. The loss is smooth: when the right class goes from a chance of 0.30 to 0.31, the loss drops a little, even though the picture is still classified wrongly. That small drop is the signal the network follows. The loss also punishes being confidently wrong much harder than being unsure, which pushes the network towards honest confidence.

Gradient descent: walking downhill in fog

Imagine standing on a hilly landscape in thick fog, wanting to reach the lowest valley. I cannot see the valley. But I can feel the slope under my feet, so I take a small step in the direction that goes down most steeply, feel the slope again, and repeat.

The landscape is the loss. My position is the current value of all 80,604 weights. The slope under my feet is called the gradient: for every weight, one number that says how much the loss would rise if that weight grew a little. A positive gradient means "making this weight bigger makes things worse", so I make it smaller. The update for every weight is:

new weight = old weight - learning rate x gradient of that weight

The learning rate is the step size. Mine is 0.01. Too small, and training crawls. Too large, and each step jumps over the valley to the other side, and the loss bounces or blows up to "nan" (not a number, the sign that the numbers overflowed).

Momentum: a rolling ball

Plain steps are jittery, because the slope measured on one small batch of pictures is a noisy estimate of the true slope. Momentum smooths this. Instead of stepping straight along the newest gradient, I keep a running direction called the velocity, like a ball rolling downhill that keeps most of its speed:

velocity   = 0.9 x velocity - learning rate x gradient
new weight = old weight + velocity

Each step keeps 90% of the previous direction and adds a bit of the new one. Noise that points left one step and right the next cancels out. A slope that keeps pointing the same way builds up speed. This simple method is called SGD with momentum; SGD means stochastic gradient descent, and "stochastic" means the gradient comes from a random batch and not from all the data.

Why SGD with momentum and not Adam?

Adam is a popular method that adapts the step size for every weight. It needs two extra arrays per weight array and more formulas to get right. SGD with momentum needs one extra array and two lines of code, and I can explain every symbol in it. For a network this small it reached 93.5% validation accuracy in 40 epochs, so there was nothing left for a fancier method to fix.

Backpropagation: passing the blame backward

Gradient descent needs the gradient of all 80,604 weights. Backpropagation is the method that computes them all in one backward sweep. The name is short for "backward propagation of errors".

I think of it as passing blame. The forward pass runs the picture through the layers from first to last and ends with a loss. The backward pass starts at the loss and walks through the layers from last to first. Each layer receives a message: "this is how much the loss changes with each of your outputs". From that message the layer works out two things. First, how much each of its own weights is to blame; those are the gradients it stores. Second, how much each of its inputs is to blame; that becomes the message for the layer before it.

The rule behind this is the chain rule from calculus. In words: if the loss changes 3 units for each unit of B, and B changes 2 units for each unit of A, then the loss changes 3 x 2 = 6 units for each unit of A. Every layer applies this rule locally. No layer needs to know the whole network; it only needs its own input, its own output, and the blame message from the layer after it.

The backward rule of every layer

Each layer type has its own small rule. In the table, d means the blame message arriving from the layer after, and x means the input the layer remembered from the forward pass.

LayerForwardBackward: gradients of its weightsBackward: message to the layer before
Softmax + losschances p from scores; loss = -ln(p of right class)nonep minus the one-hot answer, divided by the batch size
Denseout = x times W, plus bdW = x transposed times d; db = sum of d over the batchd times W transposed
ReLUkeep positives, zero the restnoned where the input was positive, 0 elsewhere
Flattenunroll to a rownoned rolled back into the remembered shape
MaxPoolbiggest of each 2 x 2 blocknoned goes to the position that won; the other three get 0
Convslide filters, sum of productsdW = for each filter cell, the sum over all positions of (d at that output) x (the input pixel that cell multiplied); db = sum of d over each mapeach input pixel collects, from every output it fed, d times the weight that was used

Four of these deserve a few more words.

Softmax with the loss. A "one-hot answer" is a row of 12 numbers with a 1 at the right class and 0 elsewhere. The blame on the scores turns out to be simply "the chances minus the one-hot answer". In the softmax example of chapter 7 the chances were Tomato 0.659, Apple 0.242, Pomegranate 0.099. If the right answer is Tomato, the blame is (0.659 - 1, 0.242, 0.099) = (-0.341, 0.242, 0.099). In words: push the Tomato score up, push the other two down, each in proportion to how wrong it was. This neat result is the reason softmax and cross-entropy are always used together.

Dense. A weight connects one input to one output. Its blame is the input's value times the output's blame. That makes sense: if the input was 0, the weight had no effect and gets no blame. The matrix formula "x transposed times d" computes this product for all 73,728 pairs at once.

MaxPool. Only the winner of each block reached the output, so only the winner can be blamed. The three losers had no effect on the result, and their gradient is 0. The layer remembers who won during the forward pass.

Conv. It is the dense rule again, repeated at every position. A filter weight was used at every position of the output map, so its blame is the sum over all those positions of "input pixel times output blame". An input pixel was used by up to nine different output positions, so its blame is collected from all of them.

Interview tip

The two rows interviewers ask for most are MaxPool and Conv. MaxPool: "The gradient is routed only to the position that was the maximum in the forward pass; the others get zero. I store a mask of the winners." Conv: "The weight gradient is the correlation of the layer's input with the incoming gradient. The input gradient scatters the incoming gradient back through the same weights, which is a full convolution with the flipped filter." I practise both until I can say them without looking.

Batch, epoch, shuffle

I do not compute the gradient from all 1,290 training crops before each step, and not from one crop either. I use a batch of 32 crops. The gradient of a batch is the average over its pictures: a decent estimate of the true slope, at a fraction of the cost. 1,290 crops make 41 batches (40 full ones and a last one of 10), so one epoch is 41 weight updates, and 40 epochs are 1,640 updates.

Before every epoch the training crops are shuffled into a new random order. Without shuffling, every epoch would present the same batches in the same order, and if the files were sorted by class, a batch would be all apples, then all beetroots. Each step would then pull the weights towards one class and the next step would pull them away again.

Why 32 pictures per batch?

A batch of 1 gives a very noisy slope and cannot use NumPy's speed, because NumPy is fast when it handles many numbers per call. A batch of all 1,290 gives a smooth slope but only one update per epoch, so learning is slow. 32 sits in between: smooth enough, 41 updates per epoch, and a batch of 32 x 3 x 64 x 64 float32 numbers takes only 1.5 MB. Some noise even helps, because it shakes the weights out of narrow dips that would not generalise.

He initialisation: how the weights start

Before training, the weights need starting values. They cannot all be zero: then every filter in a layer would compute the same thing, receive the same gradient, and stay identical forever. They must be random, and the size of the random numbers matters.

If the starting weights are too small, the signal shrinks at every layer and almost nothing reaches the output. If they are too large, the signal grows at every layer and overflows. He initialisation (named after its author, Kaiming He) picks the size that keeps the signal about the same strength through a stack of ReLU layers:

weights = random normal numbers x sqrt(2 / fan_in)       biases = 0

fan_in is the number of inputs that feed one output number: 27 for conv1, 72 for conv2, 144 for conv3, 1,152 for dense1 and 64 for dense2. So conv1 starts with weights of typical size sqrt(2 / 27) = 0.27, and dense1 with sqrt(2 / 1,152) = 0.04. The more inputs are added together, the smaller each weight starts, so the sum stays about the same size. The 2 in the formula makes up for ReLU zeroing roughly half of the values.

Overfitting and the four fixes

Overfitting means the network has learned the training pictures themselves and not the general idea behind them. It is like a student who memorises last year's exam answers: perfect on those questions, poor on new ones. The sign is a gap: training accuracy keeps rising while validation accuracy stops or falls. This is why the validation set exists. It is a pile of crops the network never trains on, used only to watch for this gap.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

My first attempt had no augmentation and a first dense layer of 128 units (155,164 weights in total). It reached 99.6% training accuracy but only 84.2% validation accuracy, a gap of over 15 points. That is clear overfitting. With augmentation and a 64-unit dense layer, the final run reached 98.1% and 93.5%, a gap of under 5 points.

There are four standard fixes, and I used three of them.

FixIdeaWhat I did
More varied dataHarder to memorise pictures that never repeat exactlyAugmentation: every training crop is randomly flipped, shifted by up to 4 pixels and brightened or darkened by up to 15%, freshly each epoch (chapter 10)
A smaller networkFewer weights means less room to memoriseDense1 went from 128 to 64 units, halving the weight count
Early stoppingStop before memorising sets inI train all 40 epochs but keep the weights of the best validation epoch (34)
Regularisers (dropout, weight decay)Extra rules that discourage relying on single weightsNot used. The first three were enough, and each addition would be more code to check
Be careful

Because I picked the best epoch by looking at validation accuracy, the validation number is slightly flattering: it is the best of 40 tries. That is why the number on my resume is the test accuracy, 92.6%, measured once on a third pile of 283 crops that played no part in any decision. Chapter 10 explains the three piles.

The gradient check: the only real proof

I wrote the backward pass of every layer by hand. A small mistake there, such as a wrong sign or a swapped axis, does not crash anything. The network still trains, only worse, and nothing tells me why. So I needed a proof that the backward maths is right. The gradient check is that proof.

The idea uses the meaning of a gradient: it says how much the loss changes when one weight changes a tiny bit. I can also measure that directly. I pick one weight, add a tiny amount eps to it, and compute the loss. I subtract eps instead, and compute the loss again. The difference of the two losses, divided by 2 x eps, is the measured slope. This is called the central difference.

measured slope = (loss(weight + eps) - loss(weight - eps)) / (2 x eps)          eps = 0.00001

relative difference = |measured - mine| / (|measured| + |mine|)

If my backward pass is right, the measured slope and my gradient agree to many digits. I test 20 randomly chosen weights in each of the 10 weight arrays, 200 weights in total, on the real network with random input pictures. The check runs in float64 (decimal numbers stored in 8 bytes, about 16 significant digits), because in float32 the rounding noise of the measurement would be as large as the mistake I am looking for.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my run the biggest relative difference over all 200 tested weights was 4.9e-07, which means the two numbers agreed to about six digits. A wrong backward pass typically gives a difference near 1, so the margin is enormous. Five of the 200 weights first showed a larger difference and were measured again with a 10 times smaller nudge, as explained below.

Be careful

ReLU and max pooling have kinks: points where the slope changes suddenly (at 0 for ReLU, and where two numbers in a block tie for max pooling). If nudging a weight by eps pushes even one unit across its kink, the measured slope is slightly off although my gradient is correct. The first convolution layer feeds over 120,000 ReLU units, so this happens for a few weights. My check measures those again with eps ten times smaller. A kink problem disappears with the smaller nudge; a real bug stays wrong at every nudge size. Before I understood this, a failing first layer cost me an evening of hunting for a bug that was not there.

Why is this the only real proof?

A falling loss does not prove the gradients are right. A network with a partly wrong backward pass often still learns, just to a lower accuracy, and I would blame the data or the design. The gradient check compares my formulas with a direct measurement that uses only the forward pass, which is much simpler to get right. When they agree to six digits for every layer, the backward code is correct, and any remaining weakness is in the design or the data, not in the maths.

Interview tip

"How do you know your backpropagation is correct?" is the natural follow-up to "from scratch". My answer: "I ran a numerical gradient check. For 20 random weights in each layer I compared my analytic gradient with a central difference, eps 1e-5 in float64. The largest relative difference was about 5e-7. I also had to handle ReLU and max-pool kinks by re-measuring with a smaller eps." The detail about kinks shows I actually ran it.

9 The from-scratch code, explained

Short version: the whole classifier is one file, cnn_numpy.py, about 310 lines. Every layer is a small class with a forward and a backward method. The only clever part is the convolution: it loops over the 9 cells of the filter, not over the pixels, and lets NumPy's einsum handle all pictures and positions at once. This chapter walks through the file piece by piece. The complete file is in chapter 17.

The shape of the file

The file classifier/cnn_numpy.py has four parts: image preparation (chapter 6), the layers, the loss, and the network class that chains the layers. Both the training script on the laptop and the kiosk app on the Pi import this same file.

Every layer follows one pattern, and knowing the pattern makes the whole file easy to read.

MethodReceivesReturnsRemembers
forward(x)the layer's inputthe layer's outputwhatever backward will need: the input, a mask, or a shape
backward(d)the blame on its outputthe blame on its inputthe gradients of its own weights, in self.dW and self.db

Data flows as NumPy arrays of shape (N, C, H, W): N pictures in the batch, C channels, H rows, W columns. During training N is 32. At the kiosk N is 1.

The slow convolution, for understanding

I first wrote convolution exactly as chapter 7 describes it, with plain loops. This version stays in the file as a reference and as a test for the fast one.

def conv_forward_slow(x, W, b):
    """Convolution of ONE picture x (C, H, W) with plain Python loops.

    W is (F, C, k, k): F filters, each C channels deep and k x k wide.
    This is the textbook definition. It is far too slow to train with.
    """
    F, C, k, _ = W.shape
    H_out, W_out = x.shape[1] - k + 1, x.shape[2] - k + 1
    out = np.zeros((F, H_out, W_out), x.dtype)
    for f in range(F):                      # every filter
        for r in range(H_out):              # every output row
            for c in range(W_out):          # every output column
                patch = x[:, r:r + k, c:c + k]            # the k x k window, all channels
                out[f, r, c] = np.sum(patch * W[f]) + b[f]
    return out

Three nested loops visit every filter, every output row and every output column. x[:, r:r + k, c:c + k] cuts the 3 x 3 window at that position out of all channels. patch * W[f] multiplies the window by the filter, number by number, and np.sum adds the 27 products. It is the worked example of Figure 7.1 turned into code.

It is correct, and far too slow. For conv2 on one picture, the innermost line runs 16 x 29 x 29 = 13,456 times. Each run is cheap for the processor but expensive for Python, which spends most of its time on the bookkeeping of each loop turn and each small array, not on the arithmetic.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

On my laptop the slow convolution took 85 ms for the conv2 layer of one picture. The fast version below took 0.7 ms for the same job, about 120 times faster. Training with the slow version would have taken days instead of 30 minutes.

The fast convolution: loop over 9 cells, not over pixels

The rule for fast NumPy code is: let each NumPy call handle as many numbers as possible, and keep the count of Python loop turns small. So I turned the problem around.

The slow version asks, for each output position: "which 9 input pixels do I need?" The fast version asks, for each of the 9 filter cells: "which input pixels does this cell touch, over the whole picture?" The answer is simple. The top left filter cell, cell (0, 0), touches input pixel (r, c) when producing output (r, c). So over the whole picture it touches the input with its last two rows and columns removed. Cell (0, 1) touches the same block shifted one column to the right. Cell (i, j) touches the input shifted by i rows and j columns.

So the output is a sum of 9 terms. Each term is "the input, shifted by (i, j), multiplied by the weights of filter cell (i, j)". The Python loop runs 9 times, whatever the size of the picture, and each turn processes every picture, channel and position in one call.

def forward(self, x):
    self.x = x                                          # remember the input for backward
    N, C, H, W = x.shape
    k = self.k
    Ho, Wo = H - k + 1, W - k + 1
    out = np.zeros((N, self.W.shape[0], Ho, Wo), x.dtype)
    # Loop over the 9 cells of the filter, NOT over the pixels.
    # For cell (i, j): take the input shifted by (i, j) and multiply it by
    # that cell's weights for all pictures, channels and positions at once.
    for i in range(k):
        for j in range(k):
            shifted = x[:, :, i:i + Ho, j:j + Wo]       # (N, C, Ho, Wo)
            out += np.einsum("nchw,fc->nfhw", shifted, self.W[:, :, i, j], optimize=True)
    out += self.b[None, :, None, None]
    return out

self.x = x remembers the input for the backward pass. Ho and Wo are the output height and width from the formula "input - k + 1". out starts as zeros of the output shape (N, F, Ho, Wo), where F is the number of filters. Inside the double loop, shifted is the input block that filter cell (i, j) touches, and self.W[:, :, i, j] is that cell's weights: one number for every pair of filter and input channel, a small table of shape (F, C). The einsum line multiplies the two and adds the result to the output. After the loop the bias of each filter is added to its whole map.

Reading the einsum letters

np.einsum is NumPy's general tool for "multiply these arrays and add up over some axes". The string tells it how. Each letter names one axis.

"nchw,fc->nfhw"

 nchw    the shifted input:  n = picture, c = input channel, h = row, w = column
 fc      the cell's weights: f = filter,  c = input channel
 nfhw    the result:         n = picture, f = filter, h = row, w = column

Two rules explain everything. A letter that appears in both inputs (here c) means "match these axes up and multiply". A letter that is missing after the arrow (again c) means "add up over this axis". So the line says: for every picture n, filter f and position (h, w), multiply the input value of each channel c by the weight for (f, c), and add up over the channels. That is exactly what one filter cell contributes to the output. The option optimize=True lets NumPy hand the work to its fast matrix-multiplication routines.

Why einsum and not more loops or a library call?

The alternatives were a loop over channels and filters (slow), or reshaping everything into one big matrix by hand (fast but hard to read and easy to get wrong). einsum states the maths directly: the letters in the string are the same letters I use when I explain the formula. When I read the line again months later, the string tells me what it does.

The convolution backward pass

The backward pass uses the same 9-cell loop and the same shifted blocks. It computes the two things chapter 8 listed: the gradient of the weights, and the blame for the input.

def backward(self, d):
    x, k = self.x, self.k
    Ho, Wo = d.shape[2], d.shape[3]
    self.dW = np.zeros_like(self.W)
    self.db = d.sum(axis=(0, 2, 3))                     # each bias touched every output of its map
    dx = np.zeros_like(x) if self.need_dx else None
    for i in range(k):
        for j in range(k):
            shifted = x[:, :, i:i + Ho, j:j + Wo]
            # Blame for weight (f, c, i, j) = sum of (blame of output) x (input it multiplied).
            self.dW[:, :, i, j] = np.einsum("nfhw,nchw->fc", d, shifted, optimize=True)
            if self.need_dx:
                # Blame for the input = blame of every output it fed, times the weight used.
                dx[:, :, i:i + Ho, j:j + Wo] += np.einsum(
                    "nfhw,fc->nchw", d, self.W[:, :, i, j], optimize=True)
    return dx

The bias gradient. Each filter's bias was added to every position of its map in every picture. So its gradient is the sum of the blame d over pictures, rows and columns: d.sum(axis=(0, 2, 3)).

The weight gradient. In the forward pass, the weight of cell (i, j) for filter f and channel c multiplied the shifted input and added to the output. So its blame is the sum, over all pictures and positions, of "output blame times the input value it multiplied". The einsum string "nfhw,nchw->fc" says exactly that: multiply d by the shifted input where n, h and w match, add up over n, h and w, and keep one number per (f, c).

The input blame. Each pixel of the shifted block fed the output through this cell's weights, so it collects "output blame times weight" from every filter. The string "nfhw,fc->nchw" multiplies d by the weights and adds up over the filters f. The result is added into dx at the same shifted position the block was read from. The += matters: a pixel in the middle of the picture belongs to all nine shifted blocks, and its total blame is the sum of all nine contributions.

The forward and backward code mirror each other. Forward reads a shifted block and adds into the output. Backward takes the output's blame and adds into the same shifted block. If I remember the forward line, I can rebuild the backward lines from it.

Why does the first layer skip dx?

The first convolution's input is the picture. Nobody needs the blame for the picture, because pixels are not adjusted during training. Conv1 has the largest maps (62 x 62), so computing its input blame would be the most expensive einsum of the whole backward pass, done for nothing. The flag need_dx=False skips it and makes every training step noticeably faster.

ReLU

class ReLU:
    """Keep positive numbers, turn negative numbers into 0."""

    def forward(self, x):
        self.keep = x > 0
        return x * self.keep

    def backward(self, d):
        return d * self.keep            # blame passes only where the input was positive

x > 0 makes an array of True and False of the same shape as x. Multiplying by it keeps the positive numbers and zeroes the rest (True counts as 1, False as 0). The same array is used backward: blame passes only where the input was positive. Where ReLU output 0, the input had no effect on the loss, so its blame is 0.

MaxPool with the mask

class MaxPool:
    """2 x 2 max pooling: keep the biggest of every 2 x 2 block."""

    def forward(self, x):
        N, C, H, W = x.shape
        Ho, Wo = H // 2, W // 2                              # an odd last row/column is dropped
        self.in_shape = x.shape
        blocks = x[:, :, :2 * Ho, :2 * Wo].reshape(N, C, Ho, 2, Wo, 2)
        blocks = blocks.transpose(0, 1, 2, 4, 3, 5).reshape(N, C, Ho, Wo, 4)   # 4 numbers per block
        winner = blocks.argmax(axis=4)                       # which of the 4 was biggest
        self.mask = np.arange(4) == winner[..., None]        # True at the winner only
        return blocks.max(axis=4)

    def backward(self, d):
        N, C, H, W = self.in_shape
        Ho, Wo = H // 2, W // 2
        blocks = self.mask * d[..., None]                    # all blame to the winner, 0 to the rest
        blocks = blocks.reshape(N, C, Ho, Wo, 2, 2).transpose(0, 1, 2, 4, 3, 5)
        dx = np.zeros(self.in_shape, d.dtype)
        dx[:, :, :2 * Ho, :2 * Wo] = blocks.reshape(N, C, 2 * Ho, 2 * Wo)
        return dx

The forward pass uses a reshape trick instead of a loop. Take a 4 x 4 map. Reshaping it to (2, 2, 2, 2) splits the rows into 2 groups of 2 and the columns into 2 groups of 2. The transpose brings the two "group" axes next to each other and the two "inside the block" axes to the end. A last reshape joins the inside axes, so each 2 x 2 block becomes a row of 4 numbers. Then max(axis=4) takes the biggest of each block, for the whole batch in one call.

argmax gives the position (0 to 3) of the biggest number in each block. The line np.arange(4) == winner[..., None] turns that position into the mask: four True/False values per block, True only at the winner. I use argmax and not "equal to the maximum" on purpose. If two numbers in a block tie, which happens often after ReLU when a whole block is 0, "equal to the maximum" would mark both and send the blame twice. argmax picks exactly one winner.

The backward pass multiplies the mask by the incoming blame, which puts each block's blame at its winner and zeros elsewhere. Then it undoes the reshape steps in reverse order to get back to the input layout. If the input had an odd last row or column that pooling dropped (the 29 x 29 map), those pixels keep a blame of 0, because they never reached the output.

Flatten and Dense

class Flatten:
    """Unroll (N, C, H, W) into (N, C*H*W). No maths, only a new shape."""

    def forward(self, x):
        self.in_shape = x.shape
        return x.reshape(x.shape[0], -1)

    def backward(self, d):
        return d.reshape(self.in_shape)


class Dense:
    """Fully connected layer: every input is joined to every output."""

    def __init__(self, n_in, n_out, rng=None, dtype=np.float32):
        rng = rng or np.random.default_rng(0)
        self.W = (rng.standard_normal((n_in, n_out)) * np.sqrt(2.0 / n_in)).astype(dtype)
        self.b = np.zeros(n_out, dtype)

    def forward(self, x):
        self.x = x
        return x @ self.W + self.b

    def backward(self, d):
        self.dW = self.x.T @ d          # (n_in, n_out)
        self.db = d.sum(axis=0)
        return d @ self.W.T             # blame for the inputs

Flatten only changes the shape and remembers the old one. reshape(x.shape[0], -1) means "keep the batch axis, and work out the second size yourself", which gives (N, 1152).

In Dense, x @ self.W is matrix multiplication: for each picture, each of the 64 outputs is the sum of all 1,152 inputs times their weights. The backward lines are the table row from chapter 8. self.x.T @ d gives a gradient for every weight: input value times output blame, added up over the batch. d @ self.W.T passes the blame back to the inputs through the same weights.

Softmax and loss_and_grad

def softmax(z):
    """Turn each row of scores into chances that are positive and add up to 1."""
    z = z - z.max(axis=1, keepdims=True)        # does not change the answer, avoids huge exp()
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / e.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)


def loss_and_grad(scores, y):
    """Cross-entropy loss of a batch, and its gradient with respect to the scores.

    scores : (N, 12) raw outputs of the last layer
    y      : (N,) the right class index of each picture
    Returns (loss, d) where d has the shape of scores and feeds Net.backward.
    """
    N = scores.shape[0]
    p = softmax(scores)
    loss = -np.mean(np.log(p[np.arange(N), y] + 1e-12))
    d = p.copy()
    d[np.arange(N), y] -= 1.0                   # chances minus the one-hot answer
    return float(loss), d / N

Softmax first subtracts each row's largest score from the whole row. This does not change the chances, because the same factor cancels in the top and bottom of the fraction. But it makes the largest exponent e0 = 1, so np.exp can never overflow, whatever the scores are.

In loss_and_grad, p[np.arange(N), y] picks, for each picture, the chance given to its right class. The loss is the average of minus the logarithm of those chances. The tiny 1e-12 protects against the logarithm of exactly 0. The gradient is the result from chapter 8: the chances, with 1 subtracted at the right class, divided by N because the loss is an average over the batch.

Why are softmax and the loss in one function?

Separately, the gradient of the loss with respect to the chances and the gradient of softmax with respect to the scores are both awkward, and dividing by a chance near zero is numerically risky. Together they collapse into "chances minus the one-hot answer", which is simple and safe. So the network's last layer outputs raw scores, and this one function does softmax, loss and gradient in one place.

The Net class: forward, backward, step

def __init__(self, seed=0, dtype=np.float32):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    self.layers = [
        Conv(3, 8, rng=rng, dtype=dtype, need_dx=False), ReLU(), MaxPool(),   # -> 8 x 31 x 31
        Conv(8, 16, rng=rng, dtype=dtype), ReLU(), MaxPool(),                 # -> 16 x 14 x 14
        Conv(16, 32, rng=rng, dtype=dtype), ReLU(), MaxPool(),                # -> 32 x 6 x 6
        Flatten(),                                                            # -> 1,152
        Dense(32 * 6 * 6, 64, rng=rng, dtype=dtype), ReLU(),                  # -> 64
        Dense(64, len(CLASSES), rng=rng, dtype=dtype),                        # -> 12 scores
    ]
    # The layers that own weights, with the names used in weights.npz.
    idx = [0, 3, 6, 10, 12]
    names = ["conv1", "conv2", "conv3", "dense1", "dense2"]
    self.named = [(n, self.layers[i]) for n, i in zip(names, idx)]
    # One "velocity" array per weight array, for momentum.
    self.velocity = {(n, p): np.zeros_like(getattr(layer, p))
                     for n, layer in self.named for p in ("W", "b")}

def forward(self, x):
    for layer in self.layers:
        x = layer.forward(x)
    return x                                 # (N, 12) scores, softmax NOT applied yet

def backward(self, d):
    for layer in reversed(self.layers):
        d = layer.backward(d)

def step(self, lr=0.01, momentum=0.9):
    """One SGD-with-momentum update of every weight, using the stored gradients."""
    for name, layer in self.named:
        for p in ("W", "b"):
            w = getattr(layer, p)                        # the weight array itself
            v = self.velocity[(name, p)]                 # its velocity array
            v *= momentum                                # keep 90% of the old direction
            v -= lr * getattr(layer, "d" + p)            # add a step downhill
            w += v                                       # move the weights, in place

The constructor builds the list of 13 layers in order, with the shapes of chapter 7 in the comments. One random number generator rng, started from a fixed seed, fills all the starting weights. A fixed seed means the same "random" weights every run, so a training run can be repeated exactly. self.named lists the five layers that own weights, with their names. self.velocity holds one zero-filled array per weight array for momentum.

forward passes x through every layer. backward passes d through every layer in reverse order. They are three lines each because every layer follows the same pattern.

step is the momentum update from chapter 8, applied to all 10 weight arrays. The operators *=, -= and += change the arrays in place, which means the numbers inside the existing arrays are overwritten and no new arrays are created. That keeps the layer and the network pointing at the same weights, and saves memory.

One training step, as used in train.py, is then four lines:

scores = net.forward(x_batch)               # pictures -> scores
loss, d = loss_and_grad(scores, y_batch)    # how wrong, and the first blame message
net.backward(d)                             # blame back through every layer
net.step(lr=0.01, momentum=0.9)             # move every weight a small step

Saving and loading the weights

def get_weights(self):
    """A dictionary of copies of the 10 weight arrays."""
    return {f"{n}_{p}": getattr(layer, p).copy() for n, layer in self.named for p in ("W", "b")}

def set_weights(self, arrays):
    for n, layer in self.named:
        for p in ("W", "b"):
            a = arrays[f"{n}_{p}"]
            if a.shape != getattr(layer, p).shape:
                raise ValueError(f"{n}_{p}: file has {a.shape}, network needs {getattr(layer, p).shape}")
            setattr(layer, p, a.astype(getattr(layer, p).dtype))

def save_weights(self, path):
    np.savez(path, **self.get_weights())

def load_weights(self, path):
    with np.load(path) as data:
        self.set_weights({k: data[k] for k in data.files})

A trained network is nothing but its weights. get_weights collects the 10 arrays into a dictionary with names such as conv1_W and dense2_b. np.savez writes them into one file, weights.npz. load_weights reads the file back and set_weights copies each array into its layer, after checking that the shape matches. If I ever change the network and try to load an old file, the check stops with a clear message instead of letting a wrong shape cause a confusing error later.

The training script also uses get_weights on its own: after each epoch with a new best validation accuracy, it takes a copy of the weights in memory, and at the end it saves that best copy.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

My weights.npz holds 10 arrays and is about 320 kB: 80,604 weights x 4 bytes each, plus a small header for each array.

Predicting

def predict(net, x):
    """Class index and confidence (0..1) for ONE prepared picture x of shape (1, 3, 64, 64)."""
    p = softmax(net.forward(x))[0]
    best = int(p.argmax())
    return best, float(p[best])


def top_k(net, x, k=3):
    """The k most likely classes for one picture, best first: [(class_index, chance), ...]."""
    p = softmax(net.forward(x))[0]
    order = np.argsort(p)[::-1][:k]
    return [(int(i), float(p[i])) for i in order]

predict is what the kiosk calls. It runs the forward pass, applies softmax, and returns the index of the highest chance together with that chance. top_k returns the best three, which the app shows when the customer taps "Wrong item" or when the confidence is below 0.60. np.argsort gives the positions that would sort the chances from small to large; [::-1] reverses that to large first.

Why float32

NumPy's default decimal type is float64: 8 bytes, about 16 significant digits. All weights and pictures in my network are float32: 4 bytes, about 7 digits.

  • Half the memory. Arrays are half as large, so more of them fit in the processor's fast cache memory.
  • Faster arithmetic. The Pi's processor can multiply four float32 numbers in one instruction but only two float64 numbers. On the Pi this is a large part of the difference between a quick and a slow forward pass.
  • Enough precision. The input pixels have only 256 levels each. Seven digits of precision are far more than the data contains.

The one place I use float64 is the gradient check, where I need to see differences in the seventh digit and beyond. The dtype argument of Net exists for exactly that.

Be careful

NumPy silently turns a result into float64 if any float64 array takes part in the calculation. One careless np.zeros(shape) without a dtype in the middle of the network would make everything after it float64 and half as fast, with no error message. That is why every array I create copies its type from an existing one: np.zeros(..., x.dtype) or np.zeros_like(x). A quick check is net.forward(x).dtype, which must print float32.

The gradient check code

The check from chapter 8 is a short script, gradient_check.py. Its core is this function:

def relative_difference(net, x, y, weights, pos, analytic, eps):
    """Nudge one weight up and down by eps and compare the measured slope with mine."""
    old = weights[pos]
    weights[pos] = old + eps
    up = loss_of(net, x, y)
    weights[pos] = old - eps
    down = loss_of(net, x, y)
    weights[pos] = old                                   # put it back
    numeric = (up - down) / (2 * eps)                    # central difference
    return abs(numeric - analytic) / max(1e-12, abs(numeric) + abs(analytic))

It changes one weight up and down by eps, computes the loss each time with the forward pass only, and puts the weight back. The measured slope is compared with analytic, the gradient my backward pass stored for that weight. The main part of the script builds the real Net in float64, runs one forward and backward pass on four random pictures, and calls this function for 20 random weights in each of the 10 arrays. A weight that differs by more than 1e-5 is measured once more with a 10 times smaller nudge, to rule out the kinks of ReLU and max pooling. The script prints the worst difference per array and ends with PASS or FAIL.

loss on random data: 6.3940
  conv1_W   shape (8, 3, 3, 3)     worst relative difference 4.9e-07
  conv1_b   shape (8,)             worst relative difference 6.5e-09
  conv2_W   shape (16, 8, 3, 3)    worst relative difference 2.1e-08
  conv2_b   shape (16,)            worst relative difference 4.2e-09
  conv3_W   shape (32, 16, 3, 3)   worst relative difference 2.2e-08
  conv3_b   shape (32,)            worst relative difference 2.0e-09
  dense1_W  shape (1152, 64)       worst relative difference 3.3e-09
  dense1_b  shape (64,)            worst relative difference 7.4e-08
  dense2_W  shape (64, 12)         worst relative difference 2.8e-08
  dense2_b  shape (12,)            worst relative difference 4.1e-08
weights re-measured with a smaller nudge (kinks): 5 of 200
biggest relative difference: 4.9e-07
PASS
Why check the real network and not each layer alone?

Checking the whole network end to end tests every layer and also the way the layers are joined: the order of the backward calls, the reshape in Flatten, the dropped row in the odd-sized pooling. A layer can be right alone and still be wired wrongly. Because conv1's weights sit at the very start, their gradient passes through every other layer's backward code, so a pass for conv1 is a pass for the whole chain.

Interview tip

I practise saying the two hardest backward rules without looking. Conv: "Forward, I loop over the nine filter cells; each adds the shifted input times that cell's weights. Backward, the same loop: the weight gradient is the incoming gradient times the shifted input, summed over batch and positions; the input gradient adds the incoming gradient times the weights back into the same shifted block." Pool: "Forward keeps the maximum of each 2 x 2 block and remembers which one won. Backward sends the gradient only to the winner." If I can say these two, I can rebuild the rest.

Design story

In version 1 the model was a black box to me: torch.hub.load fetched YOLOv5, and one line returned boxes and names. I could run it but not explain it. In version 2 there is no line in the classifier I did not write, and a test that proves the maths. The model is a thousand times smaller and does a simpler job, but I can answer any question about it.

10 The dataset, training, and what 92.6% means

Short version: I reused the 636 labelled photos from version 1 and cut every labelled item out as its own 64 x 64 crop, giving 1,850 crops of 12 classes. I split them by photo into training, validation and test piles, trained for 40 epochs with augmentation, and kept the best epoch. The network got 262 of the 283 test crops right, 92.6%. This chapter is about how that number was made fairly, and what it does and does not promise.

Where the photos came from

In version 1 my team photographed 14 kinds of Indian fruits and vegetables on the blue tray of the kiosk, from above, with the same Logitech C270 camera. We took the photos under different room lights on different days, so they are not all identical. Then we drew a box around every single item in every photo with MakeSense.ai, a free web tool for labelling images. That gave 636 photos, each with a label file.

The label files use the YOLO format, because version 1 trained a YOLOv5 detector. Each line of a label file describes one box:

class  centre_x  centre_y  width  height
9      0.512     0.433     0.181  0.236

The four numbers are fractions of the photo size, not pixels. A centre_x of 0.512 means 51.2% of the way across the 640-pixel width, so at pixel 328. To get a pixel box I multiply by 640 or 480 and move from the centre to the top left corner. The class number follows the version 1 order of 14 classes, from 0 (Apple) to 13 (Tomato).

Design story

Those 636 photos and their boxes were the most expensive part of version 1. Labelling took the team several evenings. For version 2 I changed the kind of model completely, from a detector to a classifier, but I did not have to take or label a single new photo. The boxes that once taught YOLOv5 where items are now tell my script where to cut.

From detection boxes to classification crops

A detector answers "where are the items and what are they?" for a whole photo. A classifier answers only "what is this?" for a picture of one item. My kiosk sees one item at a time, and the colour mask already finds where it is (chapter 6). So I need a classifier, and a classifier needs pictures of single items.

The script prepare_dataset.py makes them. For every photo it reads the label file, turns each line into a pixel box, cuts the box out with the 10% margin, and passes the crop through the same prepare_image function the kiosk uses. Each crop becomes one 64 x 64 training picture, labelled with its class.

Why crops instead of whole photos?

There are three reasons. One item per picture is exactly what the kiosk shows the network, so training and use match. A photo with four tomatoes gives four training samples instead of one, so 604 photos became 1,850 samples. And in a crop the item fills most of the 64 x 64 square; in a whole photo shrunk to 64 x 64, a lemon would be a blur of a few pixels.

Dropping Capsicum and Chilly

Version 1 had 14 classes. Two of them had far fewer photos than the rest: Capsicum had 15 photos and Chilly had 17. The other classes had between 30 and 90 photos each.

After splitting by photo into 70/15/15, Capsicum would have about 2 photos in the test pile and 2 in validation. A test score from 2 photos means nothing: one lucky or unlucky crop changes it by 30 points. And with about 10 training photos, the network would see too few examples to learn the class, so it would mostly guess the bigger classes instead. I removed both classes rather than ship two classes I could not measure honestly.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Capsicum gave 41 crops and Chilly 66 crops (chillies are small and lie in groups). In a trial with all 14 classes, Chilly test accuracy was 4 of 9 crops and Capsicum 3 of 6. Version 1's YOLOv5 confusion matrix also showed Chilly as its weakest class at 0.72. Dropping both left 1,850 crops in 12 classes.

Be careful

Dropping classes is a real limit, not a trick. The kiosk cannot sell capsicum or chilli. If a customer puts one on the tray, the network will still pick one of its 12 classes (chapter 7's softmax warning). The confidence rule and the "Wrong item" list help, but the honest fix is more photos of those two items, listed in chapter 20.

The 12 classes, in a fixed order

IndexClassCropsIndexClassCrops
0Apple2306Lemon160
1Beetroot1507Mosambi170
2Brinjal2808Onion140
3Brinjal White1009Pomegranate80
4Coconut12010Potato170
5Guava9511Tomato155
Total1,850

The index is the position of the class in the CLASSES list in cnn_numpy.py, and it is also the position of that class's score in the network's 12 outputs. Output number 11 means Tomato because the list says so. The trained weights are tied to this order. If I ever sorted the list differently, every prediction would get the wrong name.

Why is the order written down in one place?

Version 1's class numbers came from the YOLO training file, and the kiosk code had its own list. Two lists that must agree is a classic source of silent bugs. In version 2 the order lives only in CLASSES. The dataset script maps the old 14 version 1 numbers to these 12 by name, and the price table is looked up by name too, so nothing else depends on the numbers.

The classes are not balanced: Brinjal has 280 crops and Pomegranate only 80. Some imbalance is fine; the network still sees about 50 pomegranate crops in training. I did not resample or weight the classes, and the per-class results below show the small classes did not suffer: Pomegranate scored 100%.

Splitting by photo, into three piles

The crops are split into three piles, each with one job.

PileCropsShareJob
Training1,29070%The network learns its weights from these.
Validation27715%Checked after every epoch to choose the best epoch and to compare design choices. Never trained on.
Test28315%Used once, at the very end, to measure the final network. Never used for any decision.

The important word is "by photo". I first put the 604 photos into piles, 70/15/15 within each class, and only then cut the crops. All crops from one photo land in the same pile. That is why the crop counts are not exactly 70/15/15: photos hold different numbers of items.

Why split by photo and not by crop?

Four tomatoes in one photo share the same light, the same shadows, the same day and often come from the same batch of tomatoes. They are near-copies. If I split crops at random, one of them could be in training and its near-twin in test. The network would then be tested on pictures it has practically seen, and the test score would be too good. This is called leakage: information from the test pile leaking into training. Splitting by photo keeps every test photo completely new to the network, which is the honest situation the kiosk faces with each new customer.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

To see how much leakage matters, I also tried a random split by crop. The same training run then scored 97.2% on its test pile, 4.6 points higher than the honest 92.6%. That gap is the size of the lie a careless split would have told.

The split uses a fixed random seed (42), so every run of prepare_dataset.py produces exactly the same three piles. The script also writes the list of photos in each pile to a file, split.csv, so I can always check which photo went where.

Augmentation: new-looking pictures every epoch

Augmentation means making small random changes to each training picture each time it is used, so the network never sees exactly the same picture twice. My three changes are:

ChangeAmountWhat it teaches
Horizontal fliphalf of the timeAn item is the same item when mirrored. Seen from above, a brinjal pointing left or right is still a brinjal.
Shiftup to 4 pixels in each directionThe crop box at the kiosk is a little different from the hand-drawn one. A small shift should not change the answer.
Brightnessmultiply all values by a random number from 0.85 to 1.15The LED strip keeps light steady but not perfect; room light and camera exposure still vary a little.

Each change is random every time, so over 40 epochs each training crop is seen in 40 slightly different versions. The changes are small on purpose. I did not use vertical flips or rotations: they would also be harmless for most items, but they slowed learning in a trial and gave no gain on validation.

Why augment only the training pile?

Augmentation is a teaching tool. Validation and test exist to measure the network on pictures exactly as the kiosk will see them. Changing them randomly would make the measurement noisy and would no longer measure the real task. So validation and test crops are always used exactly as prepared.

The training run

I ran train.py on my laptop with the settings from chapter 8: SGD with momentum 0.9, learning rate 0.01, batch 32, 40 epochs, shuffling every epoch, He initialisation with seed 0. After each epoch the script measured accuracy on the training and validation piles, and saved a copy of the weights whenever validation accuracy beat its best so far. At the end it measured the best weights once on the test pile and wrote weights.npz and metrics.json.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my run one epoch took about 45 s on the laptop's processor and all 40 epochs about 30 minutes. The best validation epoch was 34, with training accuracy 98.1% and validation accuracy 93.5% (259 of 277). On the test pile those weights scored 92.6% (262 of 283 correct).

metrics.json keeps every number of the run: the accuracy and loss of every epoch, the best epoch, the test accuracy, the confusion matrix, the per-class results and the training time. The book's numbers are copied from it, and the training curve in Figure 8.1 is drawn from it.

What 92.6% says, and what it does not

Accuracy is the share of predictions that were right: 262 / 283 = 0.926. It says that on 283 crops from 91 photos the network had never seen, taken in this kiosk box under its own light, the top guess was right 92.6% of the time.

Chance level is the accuracy of guessing at random: 1 out of 12, or 8.3%. Always guessing the biggest class (Brinjal) would score 31 / 283 = 11%. The network is far above both, so it clearly learned something real.

283 is not a huge number, so the score has some wobble. A rough rule for a measured share p over n tries is that the true value is usually within two "standard errors" of it, where the standard error is the square root of p x (1 - p) / n. Here that is the square root of 0.926 x 0.074 / 283 = 0.016, so 92.6% means "most likely between about 89% and 96%". I quote 92.6% because it is the measured number, but I never claim the first decimal is meaningful.

92.6% does say92.6% does not say
The network generalises to new photos of the same 12 items in this box.That it would work in a different box, light or camera.
The test was fair: split by photo, test pile used once.That it would score the same on next season's produce or other varieties.
About 7 of every 100 items need the "Wrong item" button.That the mistakes are spread evenly: they are not (see below).
Accuracy on hand-drawn crops.Accuracy on kiosk crops made by the colour mask, which I measured separately in chapter 11.

The confusion matrix

A confusion matrix shows exactly which mistakes were made. Each row is a true class, each column is the class the network predicted, and each cell counts test crops. The diagonal from top left to bottom right holds the correct answers. Everything off the diagonal is a mistake, and its position says what was mistaken for what.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my run there were 21 test mistakes. The most confused pair was Mosambi and Lemon with 7 (4 Mosambi called Lemon, 3 Lemon called Mosambi). Then Brinjal and Brinjal White with 4, Onion and Potato with 3, and Guava called Mosambi twice. The other 5 mistakes were single ones. Tomato, Coconut and Pomegranate had no mistakes at all.

The pattern makes sense once I look at the crops myself. From above, a mosambi and a lemon are both round, both green-yellow, both about the same size, and both have a dimpled skin that disappears at 64 x 64. The main differences are that a mosambi is a little larger and greener, and size is partly lost because every crop is resized to the same 64 x 64. Brinjal and Brinjal White share their shape and their green cap and differ only in colour, and a pale purple brinjal under bright light gets close to the white variety. Onion and potato are both round and brownish with a dry, patchy skin.

The classes that never failed each have something no other class has: the deep red and green star of a tomato, the hairy brown shell of a coconut, the crown of a pomegranate.

Why look at the matrix and not only the accuracy?

One number hides where the problems are. The matrix tells me that a third of all mistakes are one pair, Mosambi and Lemon. That is useful in three ways. It tells me what to fix first (more and better photos of those two). It tells me what the shop should expect: a wrong Lemon instead of Mosambi costs the shop Rs 50 per kg. And it shaped the app: the "Wrong item" screen always shows the top 3 guesses, and for a mosambi the second guess is nearly always Lemon.

Precision and recall, for one class

Accuracy per class answers "of the real mosambis, how many did I get right?". That is called recall. There is a second question: "when the network says Mosambi, how often is it really a mosambi?". That is called precision.

MosambiCountMeaning
True Mosambi crops27the Mosambi row of the matrix
Called Mosambi correctly23the diagonal cell
Called Mosambi in total28the Mosambi column: 23 right, 3 Lemons, 2 Guavas
Recall23 / 27 = 85%of the real mosambis, 85% were found
Precision23 / 28 = 82%of the "Mosambi" answers, 82% were right

For a shop the two errors cost differently. A mosambi sold as lemon loses money for the shop (Rs 140 against Rs 90 per kg). A lemon sold as mosambi overcharges the customer. A kiosk that wanted to lean one way could do so with a different confidence rule for each class, but I kept one rule (0.60) for all classes and let the customer correct the bill.

Interview tip

When asked about accuracy, I give the number and then the matrix in one breath: "92.6% on a held-out test set split by photo, 262 of 283. A third of the 21 errors are Mosambi versus Lemon, which look almost identical from above at 64 by 64 pixels. Tomato, Coconut and Pomegranate were perfect." Mentioning the split and the main confusion shows I understand the number, not just that I have one.

What I would need to claim it in a real shop

92.6% is a good result for a project and an honest one. It is not enough evidence for a shop to rely on. To make that claim I would need:

  1. Test photos from the kiosk itself. Crops made by the colour mask in the finished box, not hand-drawn crops from version 1 photos. Chapter 11 has a first small test of this.
  2. Produce from many sources and seasons. Lemons from three shops look different from lemons from one.
  3. Many more test items. With 2,000 test crops instead of 283, the wobble shrinks from about plus or minus 3 points to about plus or minus 1.
  4. Items the network does not know. To measure how often an unknown item is wrongly given a known name with high confidence.
  5. A cost-weighted score. Counting a mosambi sold as lemon by its rupee loss, not just as one mistake.
Be careful

I never say "92.6% accurate in a shop". I say "92.6% test accuracy on held-out crops from my dataset". The second sentence is true and still impressive; the first would fall apart at the first follow-up question.

11 Running on the Pi (edge inference)

Short version: the laptop trains, the Pi predicts. The Pi loads the 320 kB weights file once at start-up into the very same layer code, and then each item costs one photo and one forward pass: about 2.5 million multiplications, 18 ms on the Pi 4, about 60 ms including the photo. If the network's top chance is below 0.60, the screen asks the customer to choose.

Train on the laptop, run on the Pi

Inference means using a trained network to make a prediction. Edge inference means doing it on the device itself, at the edge of the network, rather than sending the photo to a server. My kiosk does all of its inference on the Raspberry Pi inside the box.

Chapter 5 explained the split: training is heavy and happens once, on the laptop; inference is light and happens for every item, on the Pi. The only thing that crosses from one to the other is weights.npz. The Pi does not need the photos, the dataset, the training script, or anything that knows about gradients.

Why does the same file run on both machines?

A network is defined by two things: the code of its layers and the values of its weights. If the Pi had its own copy of the layer code, a small difference (say, a different padding rule in pooling) would make the loaded weights compute something slightly different from what was trained, with no error message. Both machines import the one cnn_numpy.py from the repository, so the forward pass on the Pi is, line for line, the forward pass that produced 92.6% on the laptop.

Loading the weights

At start-up the kiosk app builds an empty network and fills it from the file:

weights = CLASSIFIER_DIR / "weights.npz"
self.net = Net()                     # 13 layers with random starting weights
self.net.load_weights(weights)       # overwrite them with the trained ones

Net() creates the layers with He-initialised random weights, exactly as at the start of training. load_weights then replaces all 10 weight arrays with the trained values. The shape check inside set_weights stops the app with a clear message if the file belongs to a different network. From then on the network object lives in memory for as long as the app runs.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Building the network and loading weights.npz took about 0.15 s on the Pi. Importing NumPy and OpenCV took about 1.2 s. Both happen once, at start-up.

Counting the work for one item

Chapter 7 counted the multiplications of one forward pass. Here is the same table with the time each layer took on the Pi.

LayerMultiplicationsTime on the Pi 4 (predicted)
Conv1 (8 filters on 3 x 64 x 64)830,3046.0 ms
Conv2 (16 filters on 8 x 31 x 31)968,8326.5 ms
Conv3 (32 filters on 16 x 14 x 14)663,5524.0 ms
Pooling, ReLU, flattennone (comparisons only)1.0 ms
Dense1 and Dense274,4960.5 ms
Total forward passabout 2.5 million18 ms

2.5 million multiplications in 18 ms is about 140 million per second. A Pi 4 processor can do several billion simple operations per second, so the network is not using the hardware fully. Most of the gap is overhead: each of the 27 einsum calls in the three convolutions has a fixed start-up cost, and the arrays are small. For a kiosk that predicts once per item, 18 ms is far below anything a person can notice, so I did not optimise further.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Averaged over 100 runs, one forward pass of a 64 x 64 photo took 18 ms on the Raspberry Pi 4 and 4 ms on my laptop, in float32 NumPy. The first call after start-up was slower (about 40 ms) because NumPy prepares its einsum plans on first use; the app makes one dummy prediction at start-up so the first customer never sees that.

From photo to name: the 60 ms

The forward pass is not the only step. When the weight is steady the app does this:

  1. Take a photo. camera.take_photo() throws away the frames the camera has already buffered, then reads one fresh frame. A webcam keeps a small queue of recent frames; without this step the photo could show the tray from a moment ago, before the item was put down.
  2. Find the item box. find_item_box() runs the blue-tray colour mask (chapter 6).
  3. Crop and prepare. crop_with_margin and prepare_image, the same functions as in training.
  4. Predict. top_k(net, x, 3) runs the forward pass and returns the three best classes with their chances.
  5. Show. The app writes the name, the grams and the price on the screen.
Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Capture, box, prepare and predict together took about 60 ms on the Pi: about 33 ms for a fresh frame (the C270 delivers 30 frames per second at 640 x 480), 3 ms to find the box, 2 ms to prepare, 18 ms for the forward pass and about 4 ms to update the screen.

The whole timeline of one item

The 60 ms sit inside a longer story. Most of the time between "item put down" and "name on screen" is spent waiting for the scale, not computing.

Why not make the network faster?

The network uses about 1% of the 1.5 s the customer waits. Making it ten times faster would save 16 ms, which nobody would notice. If I wanted the kiosk to feel faster, the place to work is the 1.2 s settling time, for example by running the HX711 at 80 readings per second (chapter 12). Knowing where the time goes stopped me from optimising the wrong part.

Choices that keep it light

ChoiceWhat it saves
Predict once per item, when the weight is steady, not on every camera frameAbout 30 predictions per second would keep a core busy all the time and heat the Pi; one per item costs nothing between customers
A 64 x 64 inputAbout 4 times fewer multiplications than 128 x 128
float32 everywhereHalf the memory and about twice the arithmetic speed of float64 on the Pi's processor
No Python loops over pixels: 9-cell loop with einsum, reshape trick for poolingThe difference between 18 ms and several seconds per photo
Weights loaded once at start-up and kept in memoryNo file reading for each item
Inference-only use of the same classesAt the kiosk nothing calls backward, so no gradients are computed or stored
Be careful

Each layer's forward remembers its input for a possible backward pass. During training that is needed. At the kiosk it means the network keeps the last photo's intermediate maps in memory, about 0.5 MB. That is harmless on a 4 GB Pi, but it is a design cost I know about: a product version would have a separate inference mode that forgets those arrays.

What edge inference buys

On the Pi (my kiosk)On a cloud server
Speed60 ms, always60 ms of computing plus the upload of a photo and the reply: often 200 to 500 ms on shop Wi-Fi, sometimes seconds
NetworkNot needed at allNo internet means no checkout
CostThe Pi, onceA server bill every month, growing with every item scanned
PrivacyPhotos never leave the box; they are not even savedEvery photo, which may show customers' hands or faces, leaves the shop
UpdatesCopy a new weights file to each kioskChange one server and every kiosk is updated

The last row is the one honest advantage of the cloud. For one kiosk, copying one file with scp is easy. For a hundred kiosks, I would need a proper way to send updates.

The confidence rule: 0.60

The network always gives an answer. To avoid showing a confident-looking wrong answer, the app looks at the top chance. If it is 0.60 or more, the screen shows the name with the "Add to bill" and "Wrong item" buttons. If it is below 0.60, the screen says "Not sure, please choose" and shows the top 3 guesses as big buttons, plus "Other items" for the full list.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

On the 283 test crops, 266 had a top chance of 0.60 or more, and 256 of those were right (96.2%). The other 17 fell below 0.60; only 6 of them were right. So the rule sent 6% of items to the "please choose" screen and caught 11 of the 21 mistakes before they reached the bill. For 15 of the 17 unsure crops, the right class was among the top 3 guesses shown.

Why 0.60 and not 0.90?

The threshold trades two annoyances. A high threshold catches more mistakes but asks the customer to choose much more often, even for items the network got right. A low threshold rarely asks but lets more mistakes through. I tried values from 0.40 to 0.90 on the validation pile (never the test pile) and 0.60 was the point where asking more often stopped catching many more mistakes. The remaining confident mistakes are what the "Wrong item" button is for.

A first test on real kiosk photos

The 92.6% was measured on crops cut with hand-drawn boxes. At the kiosk the box comes from the colour mask, the item is freshly placed, and the photo is new. So after the kiosk was built I ran a small test of the full chain: I put items on the tray one at a time and wrote down what the screen showed.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

I placed 60 items (5 of each class, bought on a different day from the version 1 photos). The kiosk named 54 correctly at the first try (90%). 4 of the 6 wrong ones were Mosambi or Lemon. In 5 of the 6 cases the right item was in the top 3 on the "Wrong item" screen. The colour mask found the item in all 60 photos.

90% on 60 new items is close to the 92.6% on test crops, and the same pair causes most of the trouble. With only 60 items the wobble is large (roughly plus or minus 8 points), so I treat it as "it works about as well as the test said", not as a second precise number.

Interview tip

"How does it run on the Pi?" My answer: "The Pi imports the same NumPy layer code and loads a 320 kB weights file once. Inference is about 2.5 million multiplications, 18 ms on the Pi 4. I only predict once per item, triggered by a steady weight, so the CPU is idle the rest of the time. Below 0.60 confidence the screen shows the top 3 instead of guessing." Then I add that the camera frame takes longer than the network, which shows I measured the whole chain.

Design story

Version 1 ran YOLOv5x on a laptop processor and took around a second per photo, and it ran only when the user pressed "Add". A Pi could not have run it in any reasonable time. Version 2's network is a thousand times smaller because its job is smaller, and that is what made the Pi possible: one item at a time, already cropped, 12 classes.

12 Weighing: load cell and HX711

Short version: the load cell turns weight into a voltage of a fraction of a millivolt. The HX711 amplifies it 128 times and converts it to a 24-bit number. My Python code fetches that number over two wires by pulsing the clock pin 25 times and reading one bit after each pulse. The hard part is timing: if Linux pauses my program while the clock pin is high, the HX711 resets and that reading comes out wrong. I let those rare wrong readings happen and remove them in software.

From weight to millivolts, in one paragraph

Chapter 3 described the parts; here is the chain again in one place, because everything in this chapter follows from it. The tray presses on the free end of the aluminium bar. The bar bends a tiny amount, a few hundredths of a millimetre. Two strain gauges stretch and their resistance rises; two are squeezed and their resistance falls. The four gauges form a Wheatstone bridge, so instead of four tiny resistance changes I get one small voltage between its two middle points: about 0.25 mV for a 412 g tomato, about 3 mV for the full 5 kg. The voltage is proportional to the weight. That straight-line relation is what makes calibration so simple (chapter 13).

What the HX711 does

The HX711 chip has three parts inside: a programmable amplifier, a 24-bit analog to digital converter (ADC), and a simple serial output.

  • The amplifier multiplies the bridge voltage. On channel A the gain can be 128 or 64; channel B always uses 32. I use channel A with gain 128, the most sensitive setting.
  • The ADC measures the amplified voltage and gives a whole number. 24 bits give 224 = 16,777,216 different values. Because the bridge voltage can be positive or negative, the number is signed: from -8,388,608 to +8,388,607. With gain 128 and about 3 V on the bridge, one step of the number is about 1.4 billionths of a volt at the input. My calibration (chapter 13) found 431 steps per gram, so on paper the HX711 can see a change of 1/431 of a gram. In practice the noise is much larger than one step, which is why I filter.
  • The serial output hands the number over one bit at a time on two wires.
Why 24 bits when the noise is bigger than one step?

The extra bits are not about seeing a thousandth of a gram. They give range and headroom. The empty tray alone produces an offset of 123,450 counts, and 5 kg adds about 2.15 million more. A converter with fewer bits would need careful adjustment so that both the offset and the full load fit inside its range. With 24 bits everything fits easily and there are still hundreds of counts per gram left over to average.

The chip makes a new reading 10 times a second on my board, because its RATE pin is tied low. Tied high, it makes 80 per second. Inside, the chip averages the signal over each reading's time window, so the slower rate is quieter. The HX711 datasheet also gives a settling time: after a big change it takes about 4 readings at 10 per second (400 ms) before the output fully reflects the new input.

The two wires, DT and SCK

The HX711 talks to the Pi over two wires, with a protocol (a fixed set of rules for talking) that is simpler than I2C or SPI and specific to this chip.

  • DT (data, sometimes called DOUT) goes from the HX711 to the Pi's GPIO 5. The HX711 controls it; the Pi only reads it.
  • SCK (serial clock, sometimes PD_SCK) goes from the Pi's GPIO 6 to the HX711. The Pi controls it. Each pulse on it says "give me the next bit".

How one reading works

  1. Wait for ready. While a reading is being made, DT is high. When a new reading is ready, the HX711 pulls DT low. That is the signal "come and get it".
  2. 24 pulses, biggest bit first. The Pi sets SCK high, then low again. On the rising edge the HX711 puts the next bit on DT. The Pi reads DT, and the bit becomes the next digit of the number. Twenty-four pulses give all 24 bits, from the most significant bit (the one worth 8,388,608) down to the least significant (worth 1).
  3. Extra pulses choose the next reading. The number of extra pulses after the 24th sets the channel and gain for the next reading: 1 extra pulse (25 in total) means channel A, gain 128; 2 extra means channel B, gain 32; 3 extra means channel A, gain 64. I always give 25 pulses.
  4. DT goes high again after the last pulse, until the next reading is ready about 100 ms later.

Two's complement for 24 bits

The 24 bits arrive as a plain row of ones and zeros. To turn them into a signed number, the HX711 uses two's complement, the standard way computers store negative whole numbers. The rule for 24 bits is short: read the bits as an ordinary positive number from 0 to 16,777,215. If the top bit (bit 23) is 1, the number is really negative, and I subtract 224 = 16,777,216.

Bits received (24)As a plain numberTop bitSigned value
0000 0100 1001 0111 1101 1110301,0220301,022 (empty tray plus a 412 g tomato)
0000 0000 0000 0000 0000 0001101
1111 1111 1111 1111 1111 111116,777,215116,777,215 - 16,777,216 = -1
1111 1111 1111 1111 1111 000016,777,200116,777,200 - 16,777,216 = -16
1000 0000 0000 0000 0000 00008,388,6081-8,388,608 (the most negative value)

In code this is two lines: if value & 0x800000: value -= 1 << 24. 0x800000 is the number with only bit 23 set, written in hexadecimal (base 16), and & keeps only the bits two numbers share, so the test is "is bit 23 a 1?". 1 << 24 means 1 shifted left 24 places, which is 16,777,216.

Be careful

Forgetting the two's complement step gives a bug that hides well. As long as the load cell gives a positive signal, nothing goes wrong. Then one day the zero drifts slightly negative, a reading of -16 arrives as 16,777,200, and the scale suddenly shows 38,000 g. If the scale ever shows a huge number with an empty tray, this line is the first thing I check.

The read_raw() code

Here is the method in kiosk/hx711.py that does all of the above. It returns one reading as a signed whole number, or None if the HX711 never says it is ready.

def read_raw(self, timeout=1.0):
    """One 24-bit reading as a signed whole number, or None after `timeout` seconds."""
    deadline = time.monotonic() + timeout
    while not self.is_ready():
        if time.monotonic() > deadline:
            return None                            # wire loose, chip unpowered, or swapped pins
        time.sleep(0.001)
    value = 0
    for _ in range(24):                            # 24 bits, biggest bit first
        GPIO.output(self.sck, 1)                   # pulse up: the HX711 puts the next bit on DT
        GPIO.output(self.sck, 0)                   # pulse down at once: keep SCK high only briefly
        value = (value << 1) | GPIO.input(self.dt)
    GPIO.output(self.sck, 1)                       # 25th pulse: channel A, gain 128 next time
    GPIO.output(self.sck, 0)
    if value & 0x800000:                           # top bit set: a negative number
        value -= 1 << 24                           # two's complement for 24 bits
    return value

The first loop waits for DT to go low, checking every millisecond. time.monotonic() is a clock that only moves forward, even if the Pi's date and time are changed, so it is the right clock for timeouts. If a full second passes without a ready signal, the method gives up and returns None. A healthy HX711 is ready every 100 ms, so a second of silence means something is wrong: a loose wire, no power, or DT and SCK swapped.

The second loop is the 24 pulses. value << 1 shifts the bits collected so far one place to the left, making room, and | GPIO.input(self.dt) puts the new bit in the empty place. After 24 turns the bits are in the right order. Then comes the 25th pulse and the two's complement step.

The pins are set up in the constructor:

def __init__(self, dt_pin=DT_PIN, sck_pin=SCK_PIN):
    self.dt, self.sck = dt_pin, sck_pin
    GPIO.setwarnings(False)
    GPIO.setmode(GPIO.BCM)                         # use GPIO numbers, not pin numbers
    GPIO.setup(self.sck, GPIO.OUT, initial=GPIO.LOW)
    GPIO.setup(self.dt, GPIO.IN)
