GPIO.setmode(GPIO.BCM) tells the library that pin numbers mean GPIO numbers (5 and 6), not physical pin numbers (29 and 31). SCK is an output that starts low; DT is an input. Starting SCK low matters: if SCK were high at start-up for more than 60 microseconds, the HX711 would power down before the first reading.

Why a timeout returning None, and not an exception?

A missing reading is not a disaster for the kiosk. The app's tick runs 10 times a second; if one tick gets None, the filter simply keeps its last value (chapter 13) and the next tick tries again. An exception would need catching in the app anyway. Returning None makes the "no reading" case visible in the code and keeps the app running. If None keeps coming for several seconds, the app shows "Scale not responding" so a person checks the wires.

The timing trap

The HX711 has a power-saving feature: if SCK stays high for more than 60 microseconds, the chip goes to sleep. When SCK goes low again it wakes up and starts a fresh reading. This is meant for battery devices that want to switch the chip off. For me it is a trap.

My program sets SCK high, then low, in two Python calls. Normally the gap between them is a few microseconds. But Linux can pause any program at any moment to run something else: the desktop, the Wi-Fi driver, a log writer. Most pauses are short, but now and then one lasts longer than 60 microseconds. If it happens exactly between "SCK high" and "SCK low", the HX711 falls asleep in the middle of sending a number. The bits already read are fine; the rest are not, and the number comes out wildly wrong.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Each GPIO call through rpi-lgpio took about 3 microseconds on the Pi 4, so a normal SCK pulse was high for about 3 microseconds and one full reading of 25 pulses took about 0.4 ms. Over a 30-minute test with a fixed 500 g weight (18,000 readings), 15 readings were wild, about one every 2 minutes. Every one of them was removed by the median filter of chapter 13.

Why did the ESP8266 in version 1 not have this problem?

The ESP8266 is a microcontroller running one program with no operating system. Nothing else can take the processor away in the middle of the 25 pulses, so SCK is always high for exactly as long as the code says. The Pi gives me Linux, Python, OpenCV and a touchscreen, and the price is that my program does not own the processor. The HX711 library on the ESP8266 even switches off interrupts during a reading; a normal Linux program cannot do that.

Three ways to handle the trap

WayHow it worksGoodBad
Software filter (chosen)Accept that a reading is sometimes wrong. A median of the newest 5 readings throws away any single wild one.No extra parts, no extra programs, simple to explain and test on a laptopWild readings still happen; the median adds about 0.2 s of delay
pigpio libraryA background program that uses the Pi's DMA hardware (a part that moves data without the processor) to make pulses with exact timingExact pulse timing from LinuxAn extra background program that must be running; does not support the newest Pi models; more to install
A small microcontroller in betweenA board like an ESP8266 or a tiny Arduino reads the HX711 and sends the weight to the Pi over USB serialPerfect timing, as in version 1A second device with its own firmware, the very thing version 2 set out to remove

I chose the software filter because the problem is rare (about 1 reading in 1,200) and its effect is obvious: a wild reading is millions of counts away from its neighbours, so it is easy to recognise and throw away. The filter also removes ordinary noise, which I needed anyway.

Interview tip

A good question to expect: "If Linux is not real-time, how do you bit-bang a timing-sensitive protocol from Python?" My answer: "Mostly it works because the pulses are only microseconds long. When Linux pauses my program during a pulse, the HX711 resets and that reading is garbage. That happened about once every two minutes. Instead of adding hardware, I made the readings robust: a median of 5 removes any single bad reading, and the steadiness check stops a bad value from ever reaching the bill."

10 or 80 readings per second

The HX711's RATE pin chooses 10 or 80 readings per second. I kept 10.

10 per second (RATE low, my choice)80 per second (RATE high)
Noise of one readinglower (the chip averages over 100 ms)about 3 times higher
Time to a steady weightabout 1.2 scould be about 0.4 s with a retuned filter
Chance of a wild readingabout 1 in 1,200the same per reading, so 8 times as many per minute
Fits the app's tickexactly one reading per 100 ms tick8 readings per tick, needs a reading thread

At 80 per second the app would need a separate thread (a second line of work running in parallel inside the program) to collect readings, because the tick runs only 10 times a second. That is more code and more ways to go wrong, for about 0.8 s saved per item. For a first version I kept it simple. It is the first speed improvement on my list.

Design story

In version 1 the ESP8266 averaged 10 readings and returned one integer when the laptop asked for it over Wi-Fi, once per "Add" press. There was no check that the weight had stopped moving, so pressing "Add" while the item was still rocking gave a wrong price. Version 2 reads the HX711 directly, 10 times a second, and the filter decides when the weight is trustworthy. The scale got simpler to wire and more careful to read at the same time.

13 From raw number to steady grams

Short version: a raw HX711 number becomes grams with one straight-line formula, grams = (raw - offset) / counts_per_gram. Tare finds the offset; one known 500 g weight finds counts_per_gram = 431.0. The raw readings are then cleaned by a median of 5, which removes wild readings, and a weight counts as steady only when 8 cleaned readings agree within 2 g. The cost is about 1.2 s of waiting. The result is within 2 g from 100 g to 2 kg.

Tare: finding zero

With nothing on it, the scale does not read 0. The tray, the spacer and the load cell's own small imbalance all add to the raw number. On my kiosk the empty tray reads about 123,450 counts. This number is called the offset. Taring means measuring it, so that it can be subtracted from every later reading. Shop scales have a "tare" button for the same reason: to remove the weight of the container.

The kiosk tares at every start-up. It reads 20 raw readings (2 s at 10 per second) with the tray empty and takes their median as the offset. Using the median of 20 rather than one reading means a wild reading during start-up cannot spoil the zero.

Calibration: finding the slope

Knowing zero is not enough. I also need to know how many counts one gram adds. This number, counts_per_gram, depends on the load cell, the bridge voltage and the HX711's gain, so it must be measured once for each kiosk. That measurement is called calibration.

The script kiosk/calibrate.py does it in three steps, with prompts on the screen:

  1. "Empty the tray and press Enter." It reads 20 readings and takes the median: the offset.
  2. "Put the 500 g weight in the middle of the tray and press Enter." It reads 20 more and takes the median.
  3. It divides the difference by 500, prints the result, and saves offset and counts_per_gram in kiosk/config.json.
Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

In my calibration the empty tray read 123,450 counts and the tray with a 500 g check weight read 338,950 counts. So counts_per_gram = (338,950 - 123,450) / 500 = 215,500 / 500 = 431.0. Version 1 used a scale factor of 430 on the ESP8266, which agrees: same load cell, same HX711.

After calibration, every raw reading turns into grams with one line:

grams = (raw - offset) / counts_per_gram

example: (301,022 - 123,450) / 431.0 = 177,572 / 431.0 = 412.0 g
Why does one weight and a straight line work?

The bar bends in proportion to the force, the gauges change in proportion to the bending, the bridge voltage changes in proportion to the gauges, and the HX711 converts that voltage in proportion too. A chain of proportional steps is itself proportional, so a plot of raw counts against grams is a straight line. A straight line is fixed by two points: zero (the offset) and one known weight. More points would only check the line, which is what the check-weight test below does.

Be careful

The calibration is only as good as the check weight. A "500 g" weight that is really 495 g makes every reading 1% too heavy, and that error is invisible in normal use because the line is still perfectly straight. I used a 500 g weight from a kitchen scale set and checked it against a shop's scale. If the kiosk is ever moved, rebuilt or gets a new load cell, I run calibrate.py again.

Cleaning: the median of 5

Raw readings jump around a little even with a still tray: electrical noise, air movement, vibration from the table. And once every couple of minutes a wild reading arrives from the timing trap of chapter 12. The first cleaning step handles both: for every new raw reading I take the median of the newest 5 raw readings.

The median is the middle value after sorting. Here is a worked example, in grams for readability, with one wild reading:

Newest 5 readings (g)SortedMedianAverage, for comparison
412.3, 411.6, 412.2, 412.0, 412.0411.6, 412.0, 412.0, 412.2, 412.3412.0412.0
411.6, 412.2, 412.0, 412.0, 2,600411.6, 412.0, 412.0, 412.2, 2,600412.0849.6
412.2, 412.0, 412.0, 2,600, 412.2412.0, 412.0, 412.2, 412.2, 2,600412.2849.7

The wild 2,600 g reading is always at the end of the sorted list, so it is never the middle value. The median does not move. The average jumps to almost 850 g and would stay wrong for five readings until the wild value leaves the window.

Why median and not average?

An average lets every reading pull on the result in proportion to its size, so one absurd value pulls hard. A median only cares about order. As long as fewer than half of the 5 readings are wild (fewer than 3), the median is a real reading. Wild readings come one at a time, minutes apart, so the median of 5 removes every one of them. The average would also need the median's help, so I use the median first and average only later, among cleaned values.

Why 5 and not 3 or 9?

A window of 3 also removes a single wild reading, but two wild readings that fall within three readings of each other would get through. That never happened in my 30-minute test, where every wild reading stood alone, but with a window of 3 it would only take one unlucky pair. A window of 9 is safer but makes the cleaned value lag further behind a real change: the median only switches to the new weight once more than half the window holds new readings, so 5 readings (0.5 s) for a window of 9 against 3 readings (0.3 s) for a window of 5. 5 was the smallest window that removed every wild reading I recorded.

The steady rule

Cleaned values still move while an item settles: it rolls a little, the tray rocks, the customer's hand leaves it. I must not take a photo or a price during that time. So the second step asks: are the newest 8 cleaned values all within 2 g of each other? If the biggest minus the smallest is less than 2 g, the weight is steady, and the weight I use is their average.

The two numbers come from what the kiosk needs. 8 readings at 10 per second is 0.8 s of stillness, long enough that a rocking tray or a moving hand would break the rule. 2 g is a little above the noise of the cleaned values (about plus or minus 0.5 g), so a still tray always passes, but any real movement fails. The average of 8 values then smooths the last bit of noise out of the billed weight.

The WeightFilter code

Both steps live in one small class in kiosk/weight_filter.py. The app creates one filter at start-up and feeds it every raw reading.

def __init__(self, offset, counts_per_gram, median_n=MEDIAN_N, steady_n=STEADY_N,
             band_g=STEADY_BAND_G):
    self.offset = float(offset)
    self.cpg = float(counts_per_gram)
    self.band = band_g
    self.raw = deque(maxlen=median_n)       # newest raw readings
    self.clean = deque(maxlen=steady_n)     # newest cleaned values, in grams
    self.empty_count = 0                    # readings in a row with an empty, steady tray

def to_grams(self, raw):
    return (raw - self.offset) / self.cpg

def add(self, raw):
    """Feed one raw reading (or None). Returns the newest cleaned weight in grams."""
    if raw is None:                         # read_raw timed out: keep what we have
        return self.clean[-1] if self.clean else 0.0
    self.raw.append(raw)
    g = self.to_grams(median(self.raw))
    self.clean.append(g)
    self._auto_retare()
    return g

@property
def steady(self):
    return len(self.clean) == self.clean.maxlen and max(self.clean) - min(self.clean) < self.band

@property
def weight(self):
    """The average of the newest cleaned values: the number I bill."""
    return sum(self.clean) / len(self.clean) if self.clean else 0.0

A deque with maxlen is a list that keeps only its newest items: adding a sixth item to a deque of length 5 drops the oldest. So self.raw always holds the newest 5 raw readings and self.clean the newest 8 cleaned values, with no extra bookkeeping. add appends the raw reading, converts the median of the window to grams, appends that to the cleaned list and returns it. A None from a timed-out reading changes nothing. steady and weight are properties, which means the app reads them like plain values (f.steady) although they are computed fresh each time.

The file starts with a small doctest, an example written like a Python session that Python can run and check by itself. Running python3 weight_filter.py checks it and then plays a made-up run with my calibration numbers, including the tomato landing and one wild reading. Figure 13.2 is drawn from that run.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

On the real kiosk, over 50 placements of items from 80 g to 1.5 kg, the first steady value came on average 1.2 s after the item touched the tray (fastest 1.0 s, slowest 2.1 s for a rolling lemon).

The cost: 1.2 s of waiting

The filter is not free. The 1.2 s from Figure 13.2 has three parts: about 0.3 s for the median to switch to the new weight (3 of the 5 readings must be new), about 0.1 s for the tray to stop wobbling in this example, and then 0.8 s for 8 cleaned readings to agree. The last part dominates and it is deliberate: it is the stillness I demand before trusting the weight.

I accept the delay because the opposite mistake is worse. A price taken while the item is still rocking is wrong, and a customer who sees a wrong price stops trusting the kiosk. 1.2 s feels like a natural pause, about the time it takes to let go of the item and look at the screen.

Zero drift and automatic re-tare

Over time the empty-tray reading creeps. Temperature changes the gauges and the HX711 a little, and the bar slowly relaxes. This slow change of zero is called drift.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

With the tray left empty for 4 hours, the zero drifted by about 1 g per hour, most of it in the first hour after switching on, while the Pi and the HX711 warmed up.

1 g per hour is small, but over a working day it would add up to several grams. So the filter re-tares by itself. When the tray has been empty and steady for 5 s (50 readings with a steady weight below 10 g), the current median becomes the new offset.

def _auto_retare(self):
    if self.steady and abs(self.weight) < ITEM_OFF_G:
        self.empty_count += 1
        if self.empty_count >= RETARE_AFTER:
            self.offset = float(median(self.raw))   # the tray is empty: this is zero now
            self.clean.clear()
            self.empty_count = 0
    else:
        self.empty_count = 0
Why only when empty AND steady for 5 s?

Re-taring with an item on the tray would make that item weigh nothing, which would be a costly bug. The rule needs a weight below 10 g (the "item removed" threshold) and steadiness for a full 5 s, a state that only happens when the tray is truly empty and nobody is touching it. Between customers this happens all the time, so the zero stays fresh without anyone pressing a button.

Two more thresholds come from the same thinking. The app considers an item present when the cleaned weight rises above 20 g, and removed when it falls below 10 g. The gap between them stops the app from flickering between "item" and "no item" when the weight hovers around one number. This trick is called hysteresis.

Other things that make a scale wrong

ProblemWhat happensWhat I saw (predicted)What I did
Off-centre loadAn item at the edge of the tray twists the bar slightly and reads a little high or lowA 500 g weight at each corner read between 498.4 g and 501.6 gThe tray is centred on the cell and only 25 cm wide; the screen asks to place items in the middle
CreepUnder a constant load the bar keeps bending very slowlyA 2 kg load left for 10 minutes read 0.8 g more at the endNothing: an item stays on the tray for a few seconds, so creep never matters
VibrationTapping the table or a passing trolley shakes the trayReadings jumped by up to 15 g for half a secondThe steady rule waits until it stops; no price is taken while it shakes
Something touching the trayPart of the weight goes into the frameA cable resting on the tray made a 500 g weight read 470 gThe 5 mm gap and cable routing of chapter 4
TemperatureZero moves as the electronics warm upAbout 1 g per hourAutomatic re-tare

How accurate is it? The check-weight test

Finally I tested the finished scale with known weights from a kitchen weight set. Each weight was placed in the middle of the tray five times; I recorded the steady weight each time and took the average.

Check weightKiosk reading (average of 5)Error
100 g100.6 g+0.6 g
500 g499.2 g-0.8 g
1,000 g1,002.0 g+2.0 g
2,000 g1,998.6 g-1.4 g
Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Across 100 g, 500 g, 1 kg and 2 kg check weights the error was within 2 g everywhere, the worst being +2.0 g at 1 kg. At the price of the dearest item (Apple, Rs 240 per kg), 2 g is about 48 paise.

Be careful

Within 2 g is good for a hobby scale and fine for a project demo. It is not legal for trade. In India a scale used to sell goods by weight must be an approved, verified and stamped instrument under the Legal Metrology rules. My kiosk is a working prototype of the idea, and chapter 20 lists "a certified scale" as a requirement for a real shop.

Interview tip

"How did you get stable weights?" My answer: "Three steps. Calibrate with tare plus one known weight, a straight line, 431 counts per gram. Clean every reading with a median of the last 5, which removes the wild readings from the Linux timing problem. Then call the weight steady only when the last 8 cleaned values agree within 2 grams, and bill their average. It costs about 1.2 seconds per item, and the scale is within 2 grams from 100 grams to 2 kilos."

Design story

Version 1 had no calibration file: the factor 430 was typed into the ESP8266 firmware, and the scale was tared only when the ESP8266 started. If the zero drifted during a demo, the only fix was to restart the board. Version 2 keeps offset and counts_per_gram in config.json, written by a calibration script, and re-tares by itself between customers.

14 Price, bill and the touchscreen app

Short version: prices come from a small CSV file. Every price is worked out and added in whole paise, never in decimal rupees, so the total is always exact. The app is a Tkinter window that runs a short function, the tick, 10 times a second. The tick reads the scale once and moves the app through five states: Waiting, Settling, Identifying, Showing and Remove. The weight starts the camera, and a "Wrong item" button lets the customer fix the 7 in 100 items the network gets wrong.

The price table: prices.csv

The price of each item, in rupees per kilogram, lives in a plain text file, kiosk/prices.csv. CSV means comma-separated values: one line per row, with commas between the columns. Any spreadsheet program can open and edit it.

item,rupees_per_kg
Apple,240
Beetroot,50
Brinjal,40
Brinjal White,40
Coconut,80
Guava,80
Lemon,90
Mosambi,140
Onion,25
Pomegranate,210
Potato,60
Tomato,35

The prices are the ones from version 1's price file, without Capsicum and Chilly. The function load_prices reads the file into a Python dictionary, so prices["Tomato"] gives 35. At start-up, check_prices makes sure every one of the 12 classes has a price. If one is missing, the app stops at once with a clear message, instead of crashing later in front of a customer.

def load_prices(path):
    """Read prices.csv into a dictionary {"Tomato": 35, ...} of whole rupees per kg."""
    prices = {}
    with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for row in csv.DictReader(f):
            prices[row["item"].strip()] = int(row["rupees_per_kg"])
    return prices


def check_prices(prices, class_names):
    """Stop at start-up if any class the network knows has no price."""
    missing = [name for name in class_names if name not in prices]
    if missing:
        raise ValueError(f"prices.csv has no price for: {', '.join(missing)}")
Why a file and not prices in the code?

Prices change every week in a vegetable shop. The person who changes them should not need to edit Python. With a CSV file, a shopkeeper can change "35" to "40" in any text editor or spreadsheet, and the kiosk uses the new price after a restart. It also keeps the code the same in every shop: only the price file differs.

Prices are whole rupees per kilogram. That matches how produce is priced in Indian shops, and it keeps every later calculation in whole numbers.

Money in paise, and why

A paisa is one hundredth of a rupee. Instead of storing "Rs 14.42", the kiosk stores the whole number 1,442. Every price is calculated in paise, every sum is a sum of paise, and the only time rupees appear is when a number is printed on the screen.

The float trap

Computers store decimal numbers such as 8.78 as floats: binary fractions with a fixed number of digits. Most decimal fractions cannot be written exactly in binary, just as 1/3 cannot be written exactly in decimal (0.3333...). So 8.78 is stored as a number extremely close to 8.78, but not equal to it. The tiny errors are invisible one at a time, and then show up when numbers are added:

>>> 0.1 + 0.2
0.30000000000000004
>>> 8.78 + 6.3                 # a beetroot and an onion
15.079999999999998
>>> 14.42 + 12.15 + 31.2       # three bill lines
57.769999999999996

Printed with two decimals, 15.079999999999998 still shows as 15.08, so the bug hides. But comparisons fail ("is the total equal to 15.08?" says no), and in a long day of bills rounding each line in different places can make the till disagree with the bills by a paisa here and there. In accounting, a total that is off by one paisa is a wrong total.

Why whole paise fix it

Whole numbers are stored exactly. 878 + 630 is exactly 1,508, every time, on every computer. By turning each line into whole paise once, at the moment it is priced, and adding only whole numbers from then on, the total can never drift. This is how banks and payment systems handle money too: in the smallest unit, as whole numbers.

The price formula

A price per kilogram times a weight in grams, in paise, is:

paise = grams x rupees_per_kg x 100 / 1000

412 g of tomato at Rs 35/kg:   412 x 35 x 100 / 1000 = 1,442 paise = Rs 14.42

The "x 100" turns rupees into paise and the "/ 1000" turns kilograms into grams. The weight is first rounded to whole grams, because the bill shows whole grams and should charge exactly what it shows. The result can still end in half a paisa (for example 1,033 g of onion at Rs 25/kg is 2,582.5 paise), so the function rounds that half up.

def price_paise(grams, rupees_per_kg):
    """Price of `grams` (rounded to whole grams) at `rupees_per_kg`, in whole paise.

    paise = grams x rupees_per_kg x 100 / 1000, rounded half up. All whole numbers.
    """
    g = int(round(grams))                     # the bill shows and charges whole grams
    return (g * rupees_per_kg * 100 + 500) // 1000


def rupees(paise):
    """1442 -> 'Rs 14.42'. Paise become rupees only when they are shown."""
    return "Rs " + money(paise)

Adding 500 before dividing by 1,000 with // (whole-number division, which drops the remainder) is a classic way to round half up using only whole numbers. rupees prints paise as rupees by splitting them into paise // 100 rupees and paise % 100 paise (% gives the remainder), so even printing never touches a float.

Be careful

Python's own round() rounds an exact half to the nearest even number: round(2582.5) gives 2582 but round(2583.5) gives 2584. This "banker's rounding" is fair on average, but it surprises customers who learned "half rounds up" at school. My price function does not use round() for paise; it uses the add-500 trick so a half paisa always rounds up.

The Bill class

The bill is a list of lines. Each line holds the name, the whole grams, the rupees per kilogram and the price in paise.

class Bill:
    """The customer's bill: a list of lines and a total, all in paise."""

    def __init__(self):
        self.lines = []                       # each line: (name, grams, rupees_per_kg, paise)

    def add(self, name, grams, rupees_per_kg):
        line = (name, int(round(grams)), rupees_per_kg, price_paise(grams, rupees_per_kg))
        self.lines.append(line)
        return line

    def remove_last(self):
        if self.lines:
            self.lines.pop()

    def clear(self):
        self.lines = []

    @property
    def total_paise(self):
        return sum(line[3] for line in self.lines)

    def text(self):
        """The bill as lines of text, the same layout the screen shows."""
        rows = [f"{'Item':<14}{'Grams':>7}{'Rs/kg':>7}{'Rs':>9}"]
        for name, g, rpk, p in self.lines:
            rows.append(f"{name:<14}{g:>7}{rpk:>7}{money(p):>9}")
        rows.append("-" * 37)
        rows.append(f"{'Total':<28}{money(self.total_paise):>9}")
        return "\n".join(rows)

total_paise is a property that adds up the last column. It is worked out fresh every time it is read, so it can never disagree with the lines. text() lays the bill out in fixed-width columns. Running python3 bill.py checks the small examples at the top of the file and prints a sample bill:

doctest failures: 0
Item            Grams  Rs/kg       Rs
Tomato            412     35    14.42
Onion            1033     25    25.83
Apple             618    240   148.32
Lemon              97     90     8.73
-------------------------------------
Total                          197.30
total: Rs 197.30
Design story

Version 1 calculated each cost as grams * price * 0.001, rounded it to two decimals, and added the costs as floats. It usually looked right because every number was printed with two decimals. The paise design came from asking a simple question: "can the total ever be off by a paisa?" With floats the honest answer was "rarely, but yes". With whole paise it is "never".

The state loop

The app is always in exactly one state, and each state has its own screen and its own rules for what happens next. This design is called a state machine. It turns a messy question ("what should the app do when the weight changes?") into small, separate questions ("what should the Settling state do when the weight changes?").

StateScreenLeaves when
Waiting"Place an item"; "Finish" if the bill has linesweight rises above 20 g: go to Settling. "Finish": go to Bill
Settlingthe live weight, "Weighing, please wait"weight steady and 20 g or more: go to Identifying. Weight below 10 g, or steady below 20 g: back to Waiting
Identifying(lasts about 60 ms, no screen of its own)top chance 0.60 or more: Showing. Below 0.60, or no item found: Choose
Showingname, grams, Rs/kg, price; "Add to bill", "Wrong item""Add to bill": Remove. "Wrong item": Choose. Item lifted off: Waiting (nothing billed)
Choosetop 3 guesses as buttons, "Other items" for all 12an item is tapped: it is added, go to Remove. "Cancel" on the full list: go to Remove with nothing added
Remove"Take the item off", and what was just addedweight below 10 g: Waiting
Billthe itemised bill and total; "Back", "New customer""Back": Waiting. "New customer": clear the bill, Waiting

Why the weight starts the camera

The weight is the trigger for everything. The kiosk never asks "is there something in the camera's view?"; it asks "has the weight gone up and stayed still?". There are four reasons.

  1. The right moment. A steady weight means the item is down, has stopped rolling, and the hand has left. That is exactly when a photo shows the item clearly and alone.
  2. One photo per item. The CNN runs once per item instead of 30 times a second, which keeps the Pi cool and idle between customers.
  3. No false triggers. A hand waving over the tray, a shadow or a dropped receipt changes the picture but not the weight (or not by 20 g).
  4. The weight is needed anyway. The price needs a steady weight, so waiting for it costs nothing extra.

Why "Wrong item" exists

The network is right 92.6% of the time on test crops. That means about 7 in every 100 items would be named wrongly if the app simply trusted it. Two features handle this. Below a top chance of 0.60, the app does not guess at all: it shows "Not sure, please choose" with the top 3 guesses. Above 0.60, it shows its answer but always with a "Wrong item" button next to "Add to bill". Both lead to the same Choose screen: the top 3 guesses as big buttons, which for a mosambi nearly always includes Lemon, and "Other items" for the full list of 12.

Why not just make the network better?

Even a much better network would make some mistakes, and a mistake on a bill costs real money and trust. The honest design accepts that the model is sometimes wrong and makes the correction cheap: one tap, with the right answer usually already on screen. In my 60-item kiosk test, the right item was in the top 3 for 5 of the 6 wrong first answers. A human in the loop for 7% of items is a good trade for a kiosk that handles the other 93% alone.

Be careful

The "Wrong item" button also lets a dishonest customer choose a cheaper item on purpose: weigh pomegranates, tap "Wrong item", pick Onion. My kiosk trusts the customer, like many self-checkouts. A shop version would log every correction with its photo for a staff member to check (chapter 20).

Tkinter in plain words

Tkinter is the window toolkit that comes with Python. It draws windows, text labels and buttons, and it tells my program when a button is pressed. On the Pi it comes from the python3-tk package.

A Tkinter program works differently from a script that runs top to bottom. After building the window, the program calls root.mainloop() and hands control to Tkinter. The main loop waits for events (a tap, a key, a timer), runs the small function connected to each event, redraws the screen, and waits again. My code never runs "in the background": it only runs inside these short functions, called by the main loop.

The weight, though, changes without any event. To check the scale regularly, I ask Tkinter for a timer event: root.after(100, tick) means "in 100 ms, call tick". At the end of every tick, the tick asks for the next one. So the tick runs 10 times a second, forever, and between ticks Tkinter is free to handle taps and redraw the screen.

def tick(self):
    """Runs every 100 ms: one scale reading, one filter update, one state step."""
    try:
        raw = self.scale.read_raw(timeout=0) if self.scale.is_ready() else None
        self.no_reading = 0 if raw is not None else self.no_reading + 1
        grams = self.filter.add(raw)
        self.status.config(text="Scale not responding" if self.no_reading > NO_READING_LIMIT else self.note)
        self.step(grams)
    except Exception:
        traceback.print_exc()                    # keep the kiosk alive; the log shows what happened
    finally:
        self.root.after(TICK_MS, self.tick)

Each tick checks whether the HX711 has a reading waiting (is_ready()), reads it if so, feeds it to the filter and calls step, which is the state machine of Figure 14.1. The finally block makes sure the next tick is always booked, even if something in this tick fails, and the except prints the error to the log without stopping the kiosk.

def step(self, grams):
    s, f = self.state, self.filter
    if s == WAITING:
        if grams > self.on_g:
            self.go(SETTLING)
    elif s == SETTLING:
        self.big.config(text=f"{grams:.0f} g")   # live weight
        if grams < self.off_g:
            self.go(WAITING)                     # it was lifted off again
        elif f.steady and f.weight < self.on_g:
            self.go(WAITING)                     # steady but under 20 g: not an item
        elif f.steady:
            self.identify()
    elif s in (SHOWING, CHOOSING):
        if grams < self.off_g:
            self.go(WAITING)                     # taken off without adding: nothing is billed
        elif f.steady and abs(f.weight - self.item["grams"]) > CHANGED_G:
            self.go(SETTLING)                    # something was added or swapped: weigh again
    elif s == REMOVE:
        if grams < self.off_g:
            self.go(WAITING)

The classic mistake: while True

The natural first idea for reading a scale forever is a loop:

while True:                      # DO NOT do this in a Tkinter app
    grams = filter.add(scale.read_raw())
    label.config(text=f"{grams:.0f} g")

In a Tkinter app this freezes the window completely. The loop never returns to the main loop, so Tkinter never gets a chance to draw the new text or notice a tap. The screen shows whatever was there when the loop started, and buttons do nothing. The fix is exactly the tick: do one small piece of work, return, and ask to be called again.

Why does the CNN run inside the tick?

While the tick runs, the screen cannot react, so long work inside a tick is normally avoided. The photo and the CNN take about 60 ms once per item, and the screen is about to change anyway. A 60 ms pause is shorter than a blink. Moving the CNN to a separate thread would add locks and shared-state problems for no visible gain.

Identifying, in code

def identify(self):
    """One photo, one prediction. Called once per item, when the weight is steady."""
    self.state = IDENTIFYING
    guesses = None
    photo = self.camera.take_photo()
    box = camera.find_item_box(photo) if photo is not None else None
    if box is not None:
        x = prepare_image(crop_with_margin(photo, box))
        guesses = top_k(self.net, x, 3)
    else:
        self.note = "Could not see the item, please choose"
    self.item = {"grams": self.filter.weight, "guesses": guesses}
    if guesses and guesses[0][1] >= self.threshold:
        self.go(SHOWING)
    else:
        self.go(CHOOSING)

This is the only place the camera and the network are used. If the photo fails or the colour mask finds nothing, there are no guesses, and the Choose screen opens with the full list and a message. The customer can always finish the purchase, even if the camera is covered.

Fullscreen, 800 x 480, big buttons

The app fills the whole 7-inch screen with attributes("-fullscreen", True), and hides the mouse pointer, which has no use on a touchscreen. There is no title bar, no desktop and no way for a customer to reach another program. The layout has three parts: a teal bar at the top with the item count and the running total, a large middle area for the current screen, and a thin status line at the bottom for messages such as "Scale not responding".

Why big buttons and no typing?

A fingertip covers about 1 cm of screen. On a 7-inch screen with 800 x 480 pixels, 1 cm is about 50 pixels, so every button is at least 2 cm tall with large bold text and a gap around it. Typing on a small touchscreen keyboard is slow and error-prone, so the kiosk never asks for text: every choice is a button, and the item list has only 12 names.

Finish and New customer

When the customer has put down their last item, they tap "Finish" on the Waiting screen. The Bill screen shows every line with name, grams, rupees per kilogram and rupees, then the total, in the same fixed-width layout as Bill.text(). While the bill is shown, the app ignores the scale, so leaning on the tray cannot start a new item.

"Back" returns to Waiting with the bill kept, for a customer who forgot an item. "New customer" clears the bill and returns to Waiting for the next person. In this version, payment happens at the shop's counter or by the shop's own UPI code; the kiosk's job ends with a correct, itemised total.

Interview tip

Two short answers prepare most app questions. "How does the UI stay responsive while reading a sensor?" "Tkinter's main loop calls a tick every 100 ms with root.after; each tick reads the scale once and returns, so the UI never blocks. A while-True loop would freeze it." And "Why paise?" "Floats can't store 8.78 exactly, so sums drift: 8.78 + 6.3 gives 15.079999999999998. Whole paise are exact, so the total is always exactly the sum of the lines."

15 How the program is organised

Short version: the kiosk is split into small files, each with one job: the CNN, the HX711, the filter, the camera, the bill and the app that joins them. Most files can be run and tested on a laptop with no hardware. Only hx711.py and camera.py need the real Pi, and even they have stand-ins for laptop demos. The app starts in a fixed order, runs a 10-per-second tick, and treats every failure as something to show on screen, not a reason to crash.

One file per job

FileJobNeeds the Pi?Chapter
classifier/cnn_numpy.pyImage preparation, every layer forward and backward, the network, predict and top 3no6 to 9
classifier/prepare_dataset.pyTurn the version 1 photos and boxes into the three piles of 64 x 64 cropsno (laptop)10
classifier/train.pyTrain, keep the best epoch, test once, write weights.npz and metrics.jsonno (laptop)8, 10
classifier/gradient_check.pyProve the backward pass is correctno8, 9
kiosk/hx711.pyRead one 24-bit number from the HX711; a simulated scale for demosyes for the real chip12
kiosk/weight_filter.pyRaw numbers to steady grams: median, steady rule, auto re-tareno13
kiosk/calibrate.pyMeasure offset and counts per gram with a 500 g weight, write config.jsonyes13
kiosk/camera.pyTake one fresh photo; find the item box by colour; a fake camera for demosyes for the real camera6, 11
kiosk/bill.pyPrices, paise, the Bill classno14
kiosk/kiosk_app.pyThe touchscreen app: start-up, tick, states, screensno with --simulate14, 15
kiosk/prices.csvRupees per kilogram for the 12 items14
kiosk/config.jsonCalibration and settings13, 15

The settings file holds every number a shop might need to change without touching code:

{
  "offset": 123450,
  "counts_per_gram": 431.0,
  "calibrated_with_grams": 500,
  "confidence_threshold": 0.60,
  "item_on_grams": 20,
  "item_off_grams": 10
}
Why so many small files?

Each file can be understood, tested and replaced alone. If the scale misbehaves, I look at two files (hx711.py and weight_filter.py), not at one 400-line program. If I swap the webcam for the official Pi camera one day, only camera.py changes. And each piece can be explained in an interview in one sentence, which is a good test that the split is right.

Who calls whom

Start-up order

The app does its slow and risky work once, before the first screen, in a fixed order:

def __init__(self, root, simulate=False, windowed=False):
    self.root = root
    self.simulate = simulate

    # 1. Load the weights once. The first forward pass is slow, so do a dummy one now.
    weights = CLASSIFIER_DIR / "weights.npz"
    if not weights.exists():
        sys.exit(f"{weights} not found. Train on the laptop, then copy it with scp.")
    self.net = Net()
    self.net.load_weights(weights)
    top_k(self.net, np.zeros((1, 3, 64, 64), np.float32))

    # 2. Open the camera (it throws away its first dark frames).
    self.camera = camera.FakeCamera() if simulate else camera.Camera(0)

    # 3. Set up the GPIO pins for the HX711 (the simulated scale is made in step 4).
    self.scale = None if simulate else hx711.HX711()

    # 4. Read the calibration and settings, and the price table.
    self.config = json.loads((HERE / "config.json").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
    self.prices = load_prices(HERE / "prices.csv")
    check_prices(self.prices, CLASSES)
    self.threshold = float(self.config["confidence_threshold"])
    self.on_g = float(self.config["item_on_grams"])
    self.off_g = float(self.config["item_off_grams"])
    if simulate:
        self.scale = hx711.SimulatedHX711(self.config["offset"], self.config["counts_per_gram"])
    self.filter = WeightFilter(self.config["offset"], self.config["counts_per_gram"])

    # 5. Tare: the tray must be empty now.
    self.filter.tare([self.scale.read_raw() for _ in range(TARE_READINGS)])

    # 6. The window and the first screen, then 7. start the tick.
    self.bill = Bill()
    self.item = None             # the item being shown: {"grams": .., "guesses": [(index, chance), ..]}
    self.last_added = None       # the bill line just added, shown on the Remove screen
    self.note = ""               # a message for the status line, kept until the next item
    self.state = None
    self.no_reading = 0
    self.build_window(windowed)
    self.go(WAITING)
    self.root.after(TICK_MS, self.tick)
#StepTime (predicted)Why in this place
1Load weights.npz into a fresh Net; one dummy prediction0.2 sIf the model is missing, stop before touching any hardware. The dummy prediction removes the slow first call.
2Open the camera, throw away 5 warm-up frames0.6 sThe camera needs time for its exposure to settle; doing it now means the first photo is good.
3Set up GPIO 5 and 6 for the HX711under 0.1 sSCK must be low early, so the HX711 never sees a long high.
4Read config.json and prices.csv; check every class has a priceunder 0.1 sCalibration is needed for the filter; a missing price should stop the app now, not at the first customer.
5Tare with 20 readings2.0 sThe tray must be empty; this is the moment nobody is using the kiosk.
6Build the window and show "Place an item"0.3 sOnly now is everything ready to use.
7Book the first tickFrom here on the app runs only in ticks and button presses.

Together with importing NumPy and OpenCV (about 1.2 s), the app's own start-up takes about 4 s, inside the 35 s from power-on to first screen given in chapter 5.

The tick, step by step

Every 100 ms, Tkinter calls the tick. One tick does this:

  1. Ask the HX711 if a reading is waiting. If yes, read it (about 0.4 ms). If no, use None.
  2. Count ticks without a reading. After 30 in a row (3 s), show "Scale not responding".
  3. Feed the reading to the filter and get the cleaned grams. The filter also re-tares by itself if the tray has been empty and steady for 5 s.
  4. Run one step of the state machine with the cleaned grams. In Settling this updates the live weight; when the weight becomes steady it runs identify() once, which takes the photo and runs the CNN.
  5. Book the next tick in 100 ms, whatever happened above.

Button presses happen between ticks. Tkinter calls the button's function, which changes the state and draws the new screen, and the next tick continues from the new state.

Why check is_ready() instead of waiting for a reading?

read_raw() can wait up to 100 ms for the HX711 to finish a reading. Waiting inside the tick would freeze the screen for that time, 10 times a second. Checking is_ready() first means the tick never waits: if a reading is there, take it; if not, try again next tick. Both the HX711 and the tick run at about 10 per second, so almost every tick finds a fresh reading.

Shared state

Everything the app needs to remember lives in a few fields of the one KioskApp object:

FieldHoldsChanged by
statewhich of the states the app is ingo() only
filterthe newest readings, offset, counts per gramthe tick (add), start-up (tare)
itemthe weight and the top 3 guesses of the item on the trayidentify()
billthe bill lines"Add to bill", Choose, "New customer"
no_readingticks in a row without a readingthe tick
note, last_addeda message for the status line; the bill line just added, for the Remove screenidentify(), add(), cancel()

Because the tick and the button functions all run one at a time inside Tkinter's main loop, they can never change these fields at the same moment. There are no threads, so there is no need for locks. This is the biggest reason I kept the CNN inside the tick and the HX711 at 10 readings per second.

Error handling

A kiosk has no one standing next to it to read an error message in a terminal. So every failure I could think of has a planned reaction that keeps the app usable.

What goes wrongWhat the code doesWhat the customer sees
HX711 not ready for 1 s (wire loose, chip off)read_raw returns None; the filter keeps its last valuenothing for 3 s, then "Scale not responding"
One wild HX711 readingthe median of 5 ignores itnothing
Camera read failstake_photo returns None; no guesses"Could not see the item, please choose" and the full item list
No item found in the photo (for example a hand covers it)find_item_box returns Nonethe same Choose screen
Top chance below 0.60no single answer shown"Not sure, please choose" with the top 3
Any unexpected error in a tickprinted to the log with the full traceback; the next tick still runsusually nothing
The app crashes completelysystemd restarts it 3 s later (chapter 5)the first screen again, after a few seconds
weights.npz or a price missing at start-upthe app stops with a clear message in the logthe kiosk does not start; staff read journalctl
Camera missing at start-upCamera() raises an error naming /dev/video0the same: a clear message in the log
Why stop at start-up, but carry on during use?

At start-up nobody is waiting and nothing has been billed, so a missing file is best reported loudly and at once. During a sale, a customer is standing there with items half scanned. Then the right thing is to keep going with whatever still works: a broken camera still leaves the scale and the item list, so the customer can finish by choosing items by hand.

Testing the pieces alone

Most of the kiosk can be tested on a laptop, with no Pi, no camera and no scale:

  • The filter: python3 weight_filter.py checks its doctest and replays a made-up run with a wild reading (chapter 13).
  • The bill: python3 bill.py checks the paise examples, including rounding half up, and prints a sample bill.
  • The network: python3 cnn_numpy.py checks the fast convolution against the slow one; gradient_check.py checks every backward pass; saved test crops check the accuracy.
  • The camera's box finder: find_item_box runs on any saved photo of the tray.
  • The whole app: python3 kiosk_app.py --simulate --windowed (below).

Only HX711.read_raw and Camera.take_photo truly need the hardware, and they are the two smallest pieces of code in the kiosk.

The simulated-scale flag

python3 kiosk_app.py --simulate --windowed runs the full app in an 800 x 480 window on a laptop. --simulate swaps in two stand-ins: SimulatedHX711, which behaves like my real scale (offset 123,450, 431 counts per gram, a little noise, an occasional wild reading), and FakeCamera, which draws a blue tray with a coloured round "item". The keys 1 to 9, 0, Q and W put one of the 12 items on the pretend tray with a typical weight, and the space bar takes it off.

On a laptop without RPi.GPIO, hx711.py still imports, because a small stand-in object with the same function names replaces the missing library. That is what lets the app, and the book's tests, run anywhere.

This is a real test I ran, not a prediction. With --simulate, I put a 412 g tomato down, added it, took it off, then put a 1,033 g onion down and chose it on the "Not sure" screen, and tapped "Finish". The app passed through Waiting, Settling, Showing, Remove, Waiting, Choose and Bill in that order, and the bill showed Tomato Rs 14.42, Onion Rs 25.83, total Rs 40.25.

Be careful

The simulated run tests the app's logic, not the network: the fake camera draws coloured circles, which the CNN has never seen, so its guesses in a demo are meaningless. To demo recognition on a laptop, I use saved real photos instead.

Autostart with systemd

Chapter 5 showed the service file. In terms of program structure, systemd is the outermost layer: it starts kiosk_app.py after the desktop, restarts it 3 s after any crash, and collects everything the app prints into a log I can read over SSH with journalctl --user -u kiosk.service. That log is where the tracebacks from the tick's error handler end up.

Interview tip

"How is your code structured?" My answer: "One module per job: CNN, HX711, filter, camera, bill, and an app that wires them together. Only two small functions need hardware, so I test everything else on a laptop, and the app has a simulate flag with a fake scale and camera. At run time it's a Tkinter main loop with a 100 ms tick driving a state machine, no threads, and systemd restarts it if it ever crashes."

Design story

Version 1 was one 360-line file with the GUI, the HTTP request to the ESP8266, the YOLOv5 call and the price maths mixed together, and Windows paths typed into it. Testing any part meant running the whole thing with the hardware connected. Splitting version 2 into small files was what made it possible to write most of the code and the tests before the Pi was even wired.

16 Power, frame and the physical build

Short version: one 5 V 3 A supply powers the whole kiosk, which draws about 1.1 A. The MDF box from version 1 holds everything: a post carries the camera 35 cm above the tray, an LED strip beside it lights the tray, the tray rests only on the load cell, and the touchscreen stands at a slant at the front with the Pi screwed to its back. Cables are routed so that nothing touches the tray.

Power: one supply for everything

The official Raspberry Pi 5 V 3 A USB-C supply is the only power input. From the Pi, power spreads to every other part.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

With a USB-C power meter between the supply and the Pi, the whole kiosk drew about 1.1 A at 5.1 V while showing "Place an item" with the LED strip on, and briefly about 1.3 A during the photo and CNN step. The supply voltage never dropped below 5.0 V, and the lightning symbol never appeared.

Why a 3 A supply when the kiosk uses 1.1 A?

The Pi 4 can draw much more for short moments: at start-up, when all four cores are busy, or when a USB device switches on. A supply that is just big enough lets the voltage sag at those moments, which shows as the lightning symbol, a slowed processor, or a camera that disconnects. The official 3 A supply also keeps its voltage slightly above 5 V on purpose, to make up for the loss in the cable. Spare capacity is cheap insurance.

Be careful

The 5 V pin on the header is joined directly to the supply, with no fuse between them and the screen. A short circuit on the screen's power wires goes straight to the supply. I use the screen's own jumper wires, push them fully onto the pins, and never plug or unplug them with the power on.

Heat

The Pi's processor slows itself down if it gets too hot (it starts at 80 °C). The kiosk is idle most of the time and runs the CNN for 18 ms per item, so heat is not a real problem, but the Pi sits in a closed box behind a screen. The stick-on heat sinks and a few holes drilled in the MDF behind the screen keep it comfortable.

Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Read with vcgencmd measure_temp, the processor was at about 52 °C after start-up and about 58 °C after an hour of use with a customer every minute, far below the 80 °C where it would slow down.

The camera height and the light

The camera is fixed to a short arm at the top of a vertical post at the back of the box. The arm holds the camera over the centre of the tray, lens pointing straight down, 35 cm above the tray surface. At that height the C270 sees about 29 cm x 22 cm, which covers the 25 cm x 20 cm tray with a small border.

The 30 cm LED strip is stuck along the underside of the same arm, beside the camera, so its light falls straight down on the tray. Light from directly above makes only small shadows, right under each item, which keeps the colour mask's box tight.

Why put the light right beside the camera?

Light from the side throws long shadows across the tray. A shadow is not blue, so the colour mask would count it as part of the item and make the box too big. Light from the camera's own direction hides each item's shadow mostly underneath the item itself. It also makes the brightness of every photo nearly the same, which is the reason the LED strip exists (chapter 3).

The frame from version 1

The frame is the MDF box my team built for version 1. I reused it unchanged except for three things: a mount for the touchscreen, holes for cables and air, and a new shelf for the HX711.

PartSize or position
BaseMDF, 40 cm wide x 30 cm deep, 12 mm thick
Camera postat the back, 50 cm tall, with an arm reaching over the tray centre
Camera lens35 cm above the tray surface, pointing straight down
Trayblue plastic, 25 cm x 20 cm, top surface about 5 cm above the base
Load cellunder the tray centre, one end on a spacer on the base, the other under the tray spacer
Touchscreenat the front edge, leaning back about 30 degrees from upright
Raspberry Piscrewed to the four standoffs on the back of the official screen
HX711 boardon the base, between the screen and the load cell
Why mount the Pi on the back of the screen?

The official 7-inch display has four threaded standoffs on its back made exactly for a Pi. Mounting it there keeps the short DSI ribbon and the two power jumpers short and protected, needs no extra box, and puts the Pi close to the HX711 on the base, so the four signal wires stay at 20 cm. It also puts the warmest part of the kiosk behind the screen, where the air holes are.

Why lean the screen back?

A customer standing at a counter looks down at about 45 degrees. A screen standing upright would be seen at a sharp angle, with reflections from ceiling lights. Leaning it back about 30 degrees faces it towards the customer's eyes and makes the large buttons easy to tap without reaching over the tray.

The tray rests only on the load cell

Chapter 4 gave the rule; here is how the build follows it. The load cell is screwed down at one end through a 10 mm spacer, with two M5 screws into the base. The tray is screwed at its centre to the other end through a second spacer, with two M4 screws. The spacers give the bar room to bend. Around the tray there is a gap of at least 5 mm to the post, the screen and the edge of the box.

Be careful

The screws holding the tray must be the right length. A screw that goes too deep into the load cell presses on the bending part and changes its behaviour; a loose screw lets the tray rock, which the steady rule then never accepts. I tightened them firmly, then checked that the empty scale settled to within 1 g of zero after tapping the tray.

Cable routing

Every cable has a fixed path so that none can rest on the tray or pull on the screen.

  • Camera USB and LED strip USB run down the back of the post, held by cable clips every 10 cm, then along the base behind the tray to the Pi.
  • Load cell leads leave the cell on the fixed end, which does not move, and run along the base to the HX711. A lead fixed to the moving end would carry some of the tray's weight.
  • HX711 to Pi jumpers rise from the base to the Pi's header behind the screen, bundled with a cable tie.
  • The power cable enters through a hole at the back of the base, with a cable tie inside as strain relief, so a pull on the cable cannot reach the Pi's USB-C socket.
Measured value (predicted, replace with your own number after your test)

Before I clipped the camera cable to the post, it hung down behind the tray and touched its back edge. A 500 g check weight then read 470 g, and the error changed each time the cable moved. With the cable clipped, the reading went back to 499.2 g.

The order I assembled it in

The order of assembly mattered, because some parts are hard to reach once others are in place.

  1. Load cell first. I screwed the fixed end to the base through its spacer and checked with a ruler that the bar was level and its arrow pointed down. Everything about the scale depends on this one joint being solid.
  2. HX711 next, before the tray. With the tray off, the load cell's four leads are easy to route along the base and screw into the HX711's terminals. I ran hx711.py at this point and pressed the bare bar with a finger to see the numbers rise.
  3. Tray. Fixed to the free end through the second spacer, then checked for the 5 mm gap all round by sliding a folded card around its edge.
  4. Camera and light. Clamped the camera to the arm, stuck the LED strip beside it, clipped both cables down the post. I looked at a test photo to centre the tray in the picture before tightening the clamp.
  5. Screen and Pi last. The Pi goes onto the back of the screen with the ribbon and the two power jumpers, then the screen onto its slanted mount, then the four HX711 jumpers and the two USB plugs.
  6. Calibrate only after everything is fixed. Any change to the tray, the screws or the cables can shift the zero or the slope, so calibrate.py is the very last step.
Why test each part as it goes in?

A fault found right after fitting a part has one possible cause: that part. The same fault found when the whole kiosk is closed up could be any of ten things, and reaching the load cell means taking the tray off again. Running hx711.py with the tray off, then with the tray on, then camera.py, took five minutes in total and meant that when the app first ran on the finished kiosk, it worked.

What I would build differently

Three things about the physical build are fine for a prototype and wrong for a shop. MDF swells if it gets wet, and a vegetable counter gets wet; a product would use plastic or coated metal. The jumper wires on the header can be knocked loose; a product would use a small soldered board or a screw-terminal HAT. And the tray has no cover against wind or a leaning elbow; a real scale has a guard so that only the item can touch the weighing surface. None of these change how the kiosk works, but each one is a way a real shop would break it within a week.

The finished kiosk, from the customer's side

A customer sees a blue tray in front of them, a small camera and light above it, and a touchscreen to the left saying "Place an item". There are no wires on show, no keyboard and no buttons other than those on the screen. The whole kiosk stands on a counter, needs one wall socket, and needs no network to work.

Interview tip

Physical questions are often about trade-offs I can show I thought about. Two good ones: "Why does the light sit next to the camera?" (shadows fall under the items, so the colour mask's boxes stay tight) and "What did you learn from the build?" (a single cable touching the tray made the scale read 30 g light; in a weighing device, mechanics matter as much as code).

Design story

Version 1's frame held a camera over a tray and a separate ESP8266 board, and the laptop stood beside it. Version 2 kept the box and the camera post, removed the ESP8266, and brought the computer inside the kiosk by mounting the Pi behind the touchscreen. The kiosk went from "a box plus a laptop plus a phone hotspot" to one object with one plug.

17 The complete code

Short version: this chapter is every file of the kiosk, in full: four files for the classifier, eight for the kiosk, and the README. First comes a map of every function and class, then a reading guide that explains the choices that are not obvious, then the files themselves, and at the end how to run each one and what it prints. The files in this chapter were copied into the book by a script that checks they are identical to the files in the repository.

A map of the code
